The Legendre structure of the TAP complexity for the Ising spin glass
Jeanne Boursier

Abstract
. We study the complexity of the Thouless–Anderson–Palmer (TAP) free energy for Ising spin glasses with a general mixed p p -spin covariance, working with the generalized TAP functional of Chen, Panchenko, and Subag. We formulate three conjectures about the complexity (i.e. number of critical points). First, the annealed complexity is given by the Legendre transform of a variational functional constructed from the Parisi formula subject to a constraint on the overlap mass at zero, thereby establishing a precise link between the enumeration of TAP states and the large-deviation rate function of the partition function. Second, the quenched complexity is governed by the Legendre transform of a closely related functional in which the mass up to—but not including—the supremum of the support is constrained. Third, TAP states at any non-equilibrium free-energy level are organized into an ultrametric hierarchy, with ancestor states at other levels appearing only in subexponential number. Using a Kac–Rice computation combined with a supersymmetric ansatz, we establish a lower bound on the annealed complexity that matches the prediction of the first conjecture. We further extend the analysis to a conditional setting in which a hierarchical “skeleton” of ancestors is prescribed, providing additional evidence in support of the second and third conjectures.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Setting

Mean-field spin glasses are a paradigmatic source of rugged random landscapes: the interplay of disorder and high dimensionality produces an energy function with a complex hierarchy of valleys, saddles, and barriers whose geometry encodes the thermodynamic and algorithmic behavior of the model.

Let
\xi:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
be a covariance structure function satisfying Assumption
1.1
below. Given
\xi
, we consider the centered Gaussian process
\{H(\mathbf{m})\}_{\mathbf{m}\in[-1,1]^{N}}
on the full cube
[-1,1]^{N}
, defined by the covariance relation

(1.1)

\mathbb{E}[H(\mathbf{m})H(\mathbf{m}^{\prime})]=N\xi\!\left(\frac{1}{N}\langle\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\rangle\right),\quad\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\in[-1,1]^{N}.

We refer to
H
as the
Hamiltonian
of the model. The classical Ising spin glass is defined by restricting
H
to the Boolean cube
\{-1,1\}^{N}
: for spin configurations
\sigma,\tau\in\{-1,1\}^{N}
,

(1.2)

\mathrm{Cov}[H(\sigma),H(\tau)]=N\xi\!\left(\frac{1}{N}\langle\sigma,\tau\rangle\right).

The extension of
H
to the full cube
[-1,1]^{N}
will be essential for the TAP analysis developed below, since magnetization vectors—the natural coarse-grained description of the Gibbs measure—take values in
[-1,1]^{N}
rather than
\{-1,1\}^{N}
.

The Parisi formula, proved in
[
38
,
31
]
, identifies the asymptotic free energy of the Ising model as

(1.3)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{N}\log\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^{N}}e^{-H(\sigma)}\right]=\inf_{\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([0,1])}\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),

where the
Parisi functional

\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}:\mathcal{P}([0,1])\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

(1.4)

\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta):=\Phi_{\zeta}(0,0)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}t\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t,

and, for each
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([0,1])
,
\Phi_{\zeta}
solves the backward Parisi PDE

(1.5)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x)=-\dfrac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}{2}\Bigl[\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x)+\zeta\bigl([0,t]\bigr)\bigl(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x)\bigr)^{2}\Bigr],\\[4.30554pt]
\Phi_{\zeta}(1,x)=\log(2\cosh(x)).\end{cases}

The Parisi formula encodes a rich geometric structure: the Gibbs measure organizes into an ultrametric tree whose branching levels are determined by the support of the minimizing measure
\zeta
, while
\zeta([0,t])
plays the role of an effective temperature profile along the hierarchy (see Figure
1
). Recall that
\zeta
also governs the asymptotic law of the overlap
\frac{1}{N}\langle\sigma^{(1)},\sigma^{(2)}\rangle
between two independent replicas sampled from the Gibbs measure with the same disorder realization.

Rather than tracking individual spin configurations, it is natural to pass to a coarser description in terms of the
magnetization vector

\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{N})\in[-1,1]^{N}
. Informally, a point
\mathbf{m}
qualifies as a magnetization if it arises, with probability that does not decay exponentially in
N
, as the barycenter of a large collection of replicas (see Figure
1
).

Figure 1
.
Schematic tree of magnetizations. The root (center) is
0\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. A magnetization is represented by a node of the tree (an internal branching point or a leaf). A pure state corresponds to a leaf.

Individual configurations sit in pure states within bands orthogonal to the corresponding magnetizations. In the two-level hierarchy depicted in Figure
1
, the overlap between any two magnetizations—and hence between the corresponding replicas—takes only the values
0
,
q_{1}
, and
q_{2}
, where
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
are the squared distances (scaled) from the origin to the first- and second-level branching points, respectively. In this example, the Parisi optimizer
\zeta
in (
1.3
) has support
\{0,q_{1},q_{2}\}
. The weights
p_{i}
, giving the probability of landing in subtree
i
, are distributed as the cluster frequencies of a Chinese restaurant process, i.e. according to a Poisson–Dirichlet law
\mathrm{PD}(\alpha,0)
with
\alpha=\zeta(\{0\})
, and the same construction repeats recursively within each subtree. In general, the full hierarchy is described by a fragmentation of Chinese restaurant processes, in which the magnetizations play the role of tables and the replicas play the role of customers.

A central question now presents itself:
how can the magnetizations be characterized?
It turns out that they are precisely the approximate local maximizers of a random function on
[-1,1]^{N}
known as the TAP free energy. But what, exactly, is this functional, and where does it come from? A rigorous definition was given by Chen, Panchenko, and Subag
[
18
]
, who introduced
F_{\mathrm{TAP}}(\mathbf{m})
as the free-energy cost of constraining a large number of replicas to have barycenter
\mathbf{m}
. With this interpretation, it becomes essentially transparent that magnetizations must be critical points of
F_{\mathrm{TAP}}
and that their TAP free-energy values concentrate at the equilibrium level. We refer to the critical points of
F_{\mathrm{TAP}}
as
TAP states
; the magnetizations are then the distinguished TAP states whose free energy equals the equilibrium free energy. Carrying out a Parisi-type variational principle in the band orthogonal to
\mathbf{m}
, Chen, Panchenko, and Subag arrive at the following explicit definition.

Definition 1.1

(TAP free energy)
.

The TAP free energy is the random function
F_{\mathrm{TAP}}:[-1,1]^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

(1.6)

F_{\mathrm{TAP}}(\mathbf{m})=\inf_{\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})

where

(1.7)

F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=H(\mathbf{m})-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Phi_{\zeta}^{*}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\cdot)(m_{i})-\frac{1}{2}N\int_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}^{1}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t\,,

where
q_{\mathbf{m}}:=\frac{1}{N}\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}
,
\Phi_{\zeta}^{*}(q,\cdot)
denotes the Legendre transform of
\Phi_{\zeta}(q,\cdot)
, as defined in (
1.5
), and
\zeta([0,t])
stands with an abuse of notation for
\zeta([q,t])
. We denote by
\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}\in\mathcal{P}([q_{\mathbf{m}},1])
the minimizer (unique by
[
18
]
) achieving the infimum above.

Intuitively,
\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}
corresponds to the law of the overlap between two replicas sampled in the band orthogonal to
\mathbf{m}
, which explains why it is supported on
[q,1]
. The TAP free energy originally proposed by physicists
[
41
]
corresponds to the replica-symmetric case, where the minimizer
\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}
is a Dirac mass at
q=\frac{1}{N}\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}
. The optimality of
\delta_{q}
imposes additional conditions on
\mathbf{m}
that were not exploited in the original physics literature, rendering the system studied there underdetermined.

It is convenient to express the TAP free energy in macroscopic form, parametrized by the empirical measure of the magnetization coordinates rather than the magnetization vector itself. For a probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}([-1,1])
and
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
, define

(1.8)

\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\zeta):=-\int_{-1}^{1}\Phi_{\zeta}^{*}(q,m)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(m)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{q}^{1}t\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t,\qquad q:=\int m^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(m),

and

(1.9)

\mathrm{TAP}(\mu):=\inf_{\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])}\,\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\zeta).

When
\mu=\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{m_{i}}
is the empirical measure of a magnetization vector
\mathbf{m}
, one recovers the deterministic part of (
1.6
). In particular, the Parisi functional is the TAP free energy of the trivial state
\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{0}
:

(1.10)

\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)=\mathrm{TAP}(\delta_{0},\zeta).

Indeed, since
x\mapsto\Phi_{\zeta}(0,x)
is even and convex (hence minimized at
0
), we have

\Phi_{\zeta}^{*}(0,0)=-\Phi_{\zeta}(0,0),

yielding (
1.10
).

1.2.
Main results

We now state our principal results. The first concerns the
annealed
complexity of TAP critical points — the exponential growth rate of the expected number of such points at a prescribed free-energy level. The second extends the computation to a
conditional annealed
setting by introducing a hierarchical skeleton of ancestor states, which constitutes a key step toward the quenched complexity.

Clearly, the problem makes sense only if there does indeed exist a Gaussian process such that (
1.2
) holds. Thus, we work under the following assumption:

Assumption 1.1

(Covariance function)
.

The structure function
\xi:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is
C^{\infty}
, strictly convex on
[-1,1]
, and satisfies
\xi(0)=\xi^{\prime}(0)=0
.
Moreover, for every
N\geq 1
, the kernel

K_{N}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime})\;:=\;N\,\xi\!\left(\frac{\langle\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\rangle}{N}\right),\qquad\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\in[-1,1]^{N},

is positive semidefinite, so that a centered Gaussian process

\{H(\mathbf{m})\}_{\mathbf{m}\in[-1,1]^{N}}
with covariance

\mathbb{E}[H(\mathbf{m})H(\mathbf{m}^{\prime})]=K_{N}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime})

exists.

Remark 1.2
.

By a classical theorem of Schur and Schoenberg
[
34
]
, a sufficient condition for Assumption
1.1
to be met is that
\xi
admits a power-series expansion

\xi(t)=\sum_{p\geq 1}a_{p}\,t^{p}

with non-negative coefficients
a_{p}\geq 0
. To further ensure strict convexity, we keep only even coefficients, which yields the mixed
p
-spin form

\xi(t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\geq 2\\
p\ \text{even}\end{subarray}}\beta_{p}^{2}\,t^{p}.

1.2.1.
Annealed complexity

For
f\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varepsilon>0
, let
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f)

denote the number of critical points
\mathbf{m}\in[-1,1]^{N}
of
F_{\mathrm{TAP}}

satisfying
F_{\mathrm{TAP}}(\mathbf{m})\in N(f-\varepsilon,f+\varepsilon)
.

Our first main result provides a rigorous lower bound on the annealed complexity. Before stating it, we introduce a useful definition.

Definition 1.2

(Probability measures with an
(n+1)
-atom prefix)
.

Fix
n\geq 1
and set
q_{0}=0
. Given a strictly increasing sequence

(q_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
in
(0,1)
and a strictly increasing sequence of
weights
(u_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
in
(0,1)
, we write

\mathrm{Prefix}_{n+1}\!\bigl((u_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n},(q_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}\bigr)

for the collection of probability measures
\zeta
on
[0,1]
satisfying

\mathrm{supp}(\zeta)\cap[0,q_{n}]=\{q_{0},q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\zeta([0,q_{i-1}])=u_{i}\;\text{ for all }\;i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

We say that such
\zeta
has an
(n+1)
-atom prefix

\{0,q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}\}
.

Theorem 1

(Lower bound on the annealed complexity)
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{R}
,
u\in(0,1)
,
q\in(0,1)
, and

\zeta\in\mathrm{Prefix}_{2}(u,q)
.
Assume:

(i)

(Tail optimality.)

\zeta
minimizes
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}
over
\mathrm{Prefix}_{2}(u,q)
.

(ii)

(Stationarity.)

(u,q)
is a critical point of

(u^{\prime},q^{\prime})\mapsto u^{\prime}\!\left(\inf_{\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Prefix}_{2}(u^{\prime},q^{\prime})}\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta^{\prime})-f\right)
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and let
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta)
be the number of
critical points of
F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}
with

\frac{1}{N}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\in(f-\varepsilon,f+\varepsilon)
.
Then

(1.11)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta)\bigr]\;\geq\;\zeta(\{0\})\,\bigl(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)-f\bigr).

Theorem
1
is a first step toward the following conjecture, which asserts that the annealed complexity is governed by the Legendre transform of an explicit function.

Conjecture 1

(Annealed complexity formula)
.

Define

(1.12)

\Lambda(\theta)=\theta\inf_{\zeta:\zeta(\{0\})=\theta}\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta).

There exists an open subset
D\subset\mathbb{R}
containing
[\inf_{\zeta}\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),+\infty)
such that for every
f\in D
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f)\bigr]=-\Lambda^{*}(f)=\inf_{\theta}\bigl(\Lambda(\theta)-\theta f\bigr).

The right-hand side of (
1.11
) is precisely the value that the conjectured Legendre transform assigns to an optimizer
\zeta
with a two-atom prefix. A natural direction for future work is to establish the full conjecture through a purely probabilistic argument — one that bypasses the Kac–Rice machinery entirely — by connecting the counting problem to the large deviation principle for the partition function and thereby making the Legendre duality between complexity and free energy fully explicit.

The difficulty of upgrading Theorem
1
to the full conjecture via direct computation reflects a broader phenomenon in spin glass theory: variational formulas, even when available, are notoriously resistant to explicit analysis. An instructive example is the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model with
\xi(x)=\beta^{2}x^{2}
, for which the left-hand side of (
1.3
) is manifestly convex in
\beta
, yet this convexity remains entirely opaque from the variational expression on the right-hand side.

Remark 1.3
.

Recall from (
1.3
) that the equilibrium free energy is
f_{\mathrm{eq}}=\inf_{\zeta}\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)
. Conjecture
1
implies, in particular, that the complexity is strictly negative for
f>f_{\mathrm{eq}}
and vanishes at
f=f_{\mathrm{eq}}
, in agreement with the physical expectation that the equilibrium level marks the threshold above which an exponential number of metastable states ceases to exist.

Remark 1.4

(Extreme-value heuristic)
.

Let
f_{\mathrm{eq}}=\inf_{\zeta}\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)
be the equilibrium free energy. If
\zeta
denotes the optimizer in the Parisi formula (
1.3
), then

(-\Lambda^{*})^{\prime}(f_{\mathrm{eq}})=-\zeta(\{0\}).

Assuming Conjecture
1
, this means that
-\zeta(\{0\})
coincides with the slope of the asymptotic annealed complexity at
f_{\mathrm{eq}}
, which is exactly what one expects from the following extreme-value heuristics.

Denote by
\Sigma
the asymptotic annealed complexity. Recalling that

\#\{\alpha:\,F_{\mathrm{TAP}}(\alpha)\approx Nu\}\approx e^{N\Sigma(u)},

a first-order expansion around
f_{\mathrm{eq}}
(recalling
\Sigma(f_{\mathrm{eq}})=0
) gives for
y\geq 0
,

\#\left\{\alpha:\,F_{\mathrm{TAP}}(\alpha)\geq Nf_{\mathrm{eq}}-y\right\}\approx e^{-\Sigma^{\prime}(f_{\mathrm{eq}})y}.

Hence the limiting process in the shifted variable
Nf_{\mathrm{eq}}-F_{\mathrm{TAP}}(\alpha)
should be a Poisson point process (PPP) on
\mathbb{R}
with cumulative intensity

\mu((-\infty,y))=e^{-\Sigma^{\prime}(f_{\mathrm{eq}})y},\qquad\mu(\mathrm{d}y)=-\Sigma^{\prime}(f_{\mathrm{eq}})e^{-\Sigma^{\prime}(f_{\mathrm{eq}})y}\,\mathrm{d}y.

With the change of variables
x=e^{-y}
, the corresponding extremal weights
e^{F_{\mathrm{TAP}}(\alpha)-Nf_{\mathrm{eq}}}
form a PPP on
(0,\infty)
with intensity

\lambda(\mathrm{d}x)=-\Sigma^{\prime}(f_{\mathrm{eq}})x^{\Sigma^{\prime}(f_{\mathrm{eq}})-1}\,\mathrm{d}x.

By
[
40
]
, the same PPP has parameter
\zeta(\{0\})
, so
\zeta(\{0\})=-\Sigma^{\prime}(f_{\mathrm{eq}})
.

1.2.2.
Toward the quenched complexity

We now turn to the quenched complexity, that is, the typical logarithmic count of TAP critical points. We first state a conditional result that computes the complexity with a hierarchical skeleton of ancestor states fixed, and we then formulate two conjectures describing the structure of the quenched complexity.

The idea is to fix a hierarchical skeleton of ancestor states — each required to be a critical point of
F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}
at the equilibrium free-energy level — and to count descendant critical points in the orthogonal band of the deepest ancestor. For convenience, we restrict to the mixed case, but our result also holds in the pure case.

Theorem 2

(Lower bound on the conditional annealed complexity)
.

Suppose that
\xi
has at least two distinct nonzero coefficients
\beta_{p}^{2}
(mixed case). Let
n\geq 2
. Let
\bm{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})
and
\bm{q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})

be strictly increasing sequences in
(0,1)
, and let

\zeta\in\mathrm{Prefix}_{n+1}(\bm{u};\bm{q})
.
Using the notation of (
1.4
), define the
prefix free energy

\mathcal{P}^{(n)}(\bm{u};\bm{q})\;:=\;\inf_{\zeta^{\prime}\,\in\,\mathrm{Prefix}_{n+1}(\bm{u};\bm{q})}\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta^{\prime}),

and the
n
-step complexity functional at level
f

\mathcal{C}^{(n)}_{f}(\bm{u};\bm{q})\;:=\;u_{n}\,\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{(n)}(\bm{u};\bm{q})-f\bigr).

We assume:

(i)

(Tail optimality)
The infimum
\mathcal{P}^{(n)}(\bm{u};\bm{q})
is
attained at
\zeta
.

(ii)

(Stationarity)

(\bm{u};\bm{q})
is a critical point of

\mathcal{C}^{(n)}_{f}
.

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and fix an ancestor skeleton

(\mathbf{m}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)})

satisfying the admissibility conditions stated in Section
3

(Definitions
3.1
and
3.2
).

Let
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q_{n},\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)})
be the number of critical points

\mathbf{m}
of
F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}
lying in the orthogonal band of
\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)}

(and satisfying
\tfrac{1}{N}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\in(f-\varepsilon,f+\varepsilon)
). Set

\mathrm{Skel}_{n-1}=\left(\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}^{(1)}),F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}^{(1)}),\ldots,\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)}),F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)})\right).

Then

(1.13)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q_{n},\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)})\;\Big|\;\mathrm{Skel}_{n-1}=(0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),\ldots,0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta))\Bigr]\geq\mathcal{C}_{f}^{(n)}(\bm{u};\bm{q})\\
=\zeta([0,q_{n}))\,(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)-f).

Note that the conditioning on the skeleton
\mathrm{Skel}_{n-1}
also appears in
[
23
]
in the spherical setting.

Theorem
2
provides evidence for the following two conjectures, which together describe the structure of the quenched complexity.

Conjecture 2

(Quenched complexity formula)
.

Define

(1.14)

\tilde{\Lambda}(\theta)=\theta\inf_{\zeta:\zeta([0,\sup(\mathrm{supp}\zeta)))=\theta}\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta).

Then there exists an open subset
D\subset\mathbb{R}
such that for every
f\in D
,

(1.15)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\log\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f)\bigr]=-\tilde{\Lambda}^{*}(f)=\inf_{\theta}\bigl(\tilde{\Lambda}(\theta)-\theta f\bigr).

Note that it has been proved (at least in certain regimes, e.g. for the SK model slightly above criticality) that the Parisi optimizer has a jump at the top of its support; equivalently,

\zeta\bigl([0,\sup(\mathrm{supp}\zeta))\bigr)\neq 1.

See
[
6
]
.

Conjecture 3

(Ultrametric organization of TAP states)
.

For
f\in D
, let
\theta
be the optimizer in (
1.15
) and
\zeta
the minimizer
in (
1.14
) for this
\theta
.
Then:

(i)

(Ultrametric structure.)
The TAP critical points at free-energy level
f
are organized in an ultrametric tree: the overlap between any two TAP states at level
f
taken uniformly takes values in the support of
\zeta
.

(ii)

(Separation of free-energy levels.)
Whenever
f\neq f_{\mathrm{eq}}:=\inf_{\zeta}\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)
, the ancestor states in this ultrametric tree have TAP free energy
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)\neq f
, i.e., the ancestors live at a different free-energy level than their descendants.

(iii)

(Subexponential number of ancestors.)
The quenched complexity of the ancestor states is
0
: they exist, but their number grows at most subexponentially in
N
.

Remark 1.5
.

Conjecture
2
is implicit in the physics literature. In
[
3
]
, the authors observe that the supersymmetric quenched complexity of the TAP states coincides with the Legendre transform of the static free energy with respect to the largest breaking point of the overlap matrix, and that this identification yields the exact quenched complexity in the SK model as the Legendre transform of the full-RSB static free energy. We refer the reader also to
[
29
]
for related developments.

Let us explain why Theorem
2
constitutes progress toward Conjectures
2
and
3
. The connection rests on two observations. First, we expect the right-hand side of (
1.13
) to coincide with the true quenched complexity of the restricted set of TAP states descending from the skeleton. Second, good ancestors satisfying the conditioning in Theorem
2
should exist with high probability: evaluating (
1.13
) at
f=\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)
yields a vanishing complexity, which is consistent with the existence of a subexponential (but nonzero) number of such ancestor states. Moreover, this suggests that the quenched complexity is nothing but some annealed conditional complexity.

1.3.
Literature on the TAP free energy

1.3.1.
Physics background

A central challenge in the theory of mean-field spin glasses has been to identify the functional governing the law of magnetizations. An early approach was proposed by Thouless, Anderson, and Palmer
[
41
]
, who derived a set of self-consistent equations for the local magnetizations of the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model via diagrammatic expansions.

The counting of metastable states in mean-field spin glasses was initiated by Bray and Moore
[
10
,
11
]
, who counted solutions of the original TAP equations
[
41
]
for the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model, later extended to
p
-spin models by Rieger
[
33
]
. Cavagna, Giardina, and Parisi
[
14
,
12
]
then placed the computation on firmer ground by introducing a systematic Kac–Rice approach for stationary points of any index in the spherical
p
-spin model, while Cavagna, Giardina, Parisi, and Mézard
[
13
]
established a formal equivalence between the TAP and thermodynamic methods in the SK model.

The role of the Becchi–Rouet–Stora–Tyutin (BRST) supersymmetry was identified by the Rome group in a series of works
[
3
,
2
,
20
]
. A key finding of Crisanti, Leuzzi, Parisi, and Rizzo is that the BRST-invariant (SUSY) complexity equals the Legendre transform of the disorder-averaged free energy. However, their quenched computation
[
19
,
21
]
showed that this SUSY solution is unstable at every free energy level except the equilibrium one, so that it does not predict an extensive number of metastable states away from equilibrium. For the Ising
p
-spin model in the 1RSB regime, both a SUSY and a non-SUSY solution coexist and cross at the free energy where the replicon eigenvalue vanishes
[
22
]
; related phenomena were observed for spherical models
[
4
]
. The mechanism of SUSY breaking was further investigated by Parisi and Rizzo
[
32
]
, and numerical studies
[
15
]
confirmed that TAP stationary points in the SK model organize into minimum–saddle pairs compatible with the SUSY-breaking scenario.

The overall picture is rich but not fully coherent: different approaches lead to partially contradictory conclusions regarding which states should be counted, the role of supersymmetry, and whether the annealed approximation is exact. We stress that all of these computations use the original TAP equations
[
41
]
, where the overlap measure
\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}
is replaced by the Dirac mass
\delta_{q}
, leaving the system underdetermined. The present paper works with the generalized TAP free energy (
1.6
), where
\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}
is fixed by the variational principle, which resolves several of these ambiguities. For the spherical case, Kent-Dobias and Kurchan
[
29
]
have recently derived a general solution incorporating Parisi’s ground-state formula and the full RSB hierarchy.

Finally, we mention the complementary
real replica
method of Monasson
[
30
]
, in which
m
coupled clones are constrained to the same state and
m\to 1
. The connection between this approach, the tilted Parisi formula of
[
39
]
, and the Rényi entropy was recently clarified by Javerzat, Bertin, and Ozawa
[
28
]
, who showed that the Rényi complexity with index
m
coincides with the
m
-component annealed Franz–Parisi potential
[
26
]
.

1.3.2.
Rigorous approaches

The generalized TAP free energy was introduced and rigorously identified by Chen, Panchenko, and Subag
[
18
]
. The key idea is to compute the free energy cost of constraining the barycenter of a large number of replicas to equal a prescribed magnetization
\mathbf{m}\in[-1,1]^{N}
: the resulting partition function reduces to a Parisi-type variational formula evaluated on the band of configurations orthogonal to
\mathbf{m}
. This yields a TAP representation of the free energy in which the classical Onsager correction is replaced by a more general term, determined by the Parisi measure restricted to
[\frac{1}{N}\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2},1]
. The follow-up work
[
17
]
simplified the energy representation of the generalized TAP states — showing in particular that all states at the same distance from the origin share the same energy — and extended the results to zero temperature. In the spherical setting, Subag
[
37
]
developed a parallel TAP theory by defining free energy landscapes via thin bands around each point inside the sphere, and established a TAP representation for the free energy at any multi-samplable overlap.

For spherical spin glasses, the complexity of critical points was first computed (at the annealed level) by Auffinger, Ben Arous, and Černý
[
5
]
. In the pure
p
-spin spherical model (
p\geq 3
), the quenched complexity coincides with the annealed one: this was established by Subag
[
36
]
via a second moment argument and later strengthened to concentration at arbitrary energy levels by Subag and Zeitouni
[
35
]
. The success of the second moment method in the spherical case relies crucially on the rotational invariance of the model, which forces two independently sampled critical points to be nearly orthogonal. For Ising spin glasses, such orthogonality fails: even in regimes where annealed and quenched complexities are expected to agree, the second moment of the number of critical points is not controlled by the square of its mean, making the passage from annealed to quenched considerably more delicate.

A closely related line of work connects the TAP landscape to planted (or spiked) spin glass models. In such models, the disorder is tilted so that the Gibbs measure concentrates near a planted signal
x\in\{-1,1\}^{N}
; the resulting model is the Bayesian posterior for a high-dimensional inference problem. Interestingly, this model is always replica symmetric
[
24
,
7
]
. In
[
25
]
, Fan, Mei and Montanari show that the TAP complexity at the equilibrium free energy for the planted model is
0
, and it becomes strictly negative above and below that value when the spike is large enough. They also show that the planted configuration and independent replicas from the Gibbs measure fall into the same TAP state. In fact, the TAP magnetization
\mathbf{m}
matches the posterior mean (the Bayes estimator), and the mean squared error is
1-q
, where
q=\frac{1}{N}\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}
.

1.4.
Perspectives

The function
\Lambda
defined in (
1.12
) is directly linked to
the large deviations of the free energy. Indeed, as shown
in
[
39
]
(see also
[
16
]
), for every
\theta\in(0,1)
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}[Z_{N}^{\theta}]=\Lambda(\theta),

where

Z_{N}=\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^{N}}e^{-H(\sigma)}.

With a little more work, this implies that the free energy
\frac{1}{N}\log Z_{N}
satisfies a large
deviation principle on
[\inf_{\zeta}\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),\infty)
with rate
function
\Lambda^{*}
.

This provides a conceptual explanation for
Conjecture
1
in the regime

f>\inf_{\zeta}\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)
: the probability that the free energy reaches
an atypical value
f
is governed by the existence of at least one TAP
state at level
f
, and because the number of such states concentrates
around its expectation, the large deviation rate is controlled by the
annealed complexity. In other words,
\Lambda^{*}
plays a dual role — it
is simultaneously the rate function for the free energy and the
(negative) annealed complexity of TAP states. This duality is the
rigorous counterpart of Monasson’s real replica
method
[
30
]
. We will develop this connection in
a forthcoming paper.

The quenched complexity is not accessible through direct computation, as the second moment method is not expected to yield matching bounds in the Ising setting. A promising approach is to relate the quenched complexity to constrained thermodynamics: one would compute a Franz–Parisi potential
[
26
]
using classical tools and then establish that the barycenters of the constrained Gibbs measure are TAP states.

1.5.
Outline of the proof and plan of the paper

Section
2
: annealed complexity and the SUSY ansatz.

We begin with an
annealed
computation of the TAP complexity. We consider an optimizer
\zeta
of the variational problem of Theorem
1
. We count the critical points
\mathbf{m}\in[-1,1]^{N}
satisfying

(1.16)

\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}\approx\zeta|_{(q,1]}+\zeta([0,q])\delta_{q}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{N}\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}\approx q,

where
\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}
is the optimal overlap measure in the band orthogonal to
\mathbf{m}
(see Definition
1.1
). We emphasize that (
1.16
) is equivalent to
\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}([0,t])=\zeta([0,t])
for every
t\in[q,1]
.

The annealed complexity of these TAP states at free energy level
f
can be expressed as an integral over
\mathbf{m}
via the Kac–Rice formula. A direct computation, however, yields a complicated variational formula over probability measures on
[-1,1]
— representing the empirical distribution of the coordinates of the TAP states — involving many Lagrange multipliers. As observed in the physics literature
[
20
,
32
,
13
,
2
]
, there exists a distinguished local maximum of this macroscopic action at which the Lagrange multipliers cancel one another exactly. These cancellations are driven by a supersymmetry.

More precisely, the annealed complexity can be written as a partition function over bosonic variables (the magnetization and the Lagrange multipliers associated with imposing
\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=0
and
F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=Nf
) and fermionic fields (arising from the Berezin integral representation of the determinant). This action carries a BRST supersymmetry that yields Ward identities. One of these identities gives the fundamental relation

(1.17)

\langle\bar{\psi},\psi\rangle=\langle x,\mathbf{m}\rangle,

where the fermionic correlator
\langle\bar{\psi},\psi\rangle
is, up to a multiplicative constant, the subordination function of the Hessian evaluated at
0
, and the bosonic correlator
\langle x,\mathbf{m}\rangle
represents the longitudinal linear response in the direction of
\mathbf{m}
.

A crucial distinction from the physics literature must be emphasized here. The SUSY ansatz of
[
20
,
32
,
13
,
2
]
yields the identity (
1.17
) but not the value of these correlators, which was determined by an ad hoc choice. This ambiguity arose because the physicists were not working with the correct TAP free energy, leaving the system underdetermined. In our setting, the condition (
1.16
) determines the correlators uniquely, yielding

\langle\bar{\psi},\psi\rangle=N\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t.

The Ward identity (
1.17
) is then used as an ansatz to evaluate the annealed complexity. By the Kac–Rice formula, we need to compute

\int_{[-1,1]^{N}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\det(\nabla^{2}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))|\bigm|\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=0,\,F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=Nf\bigr]\\
\times\varphi_{(\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}),F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))}(0,Nf)\,\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{m})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m},

where
\mathcal{A}
is the set of
\mathbf{m}\in[-1,1]^{N}
satisfying (
1.16
) and the SUSY condition
\langle\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,\mathbf{m}),\mathbf{m}\rangle=N\xi^{\prime}(q)\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t
. The density of the Gaussian vector
(\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}),F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))
at
(0,Nf)
admits the convex-dual representation

(1.18)

\begin{split}\log\varphi_{(\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}),F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))}(0,Nf)&=-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle z,\Gamma^{-1}z\rangle\;-\;\tfrac{1}{2}\log\det(2\pi\Gamma)\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\inf_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{N+1}}\bigl\{\langle w,\Gamma w\rangle-2\langle w,z\rangle\bigr\}-\tfrac{1}{2}\log\det(2\pi\Gamma),\end{split}

where
\Gamma:=\mathrm{Cov}((\nabla H(\mathbf{m}),H(\mathbf{m})))
and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
is an explicit vector. Writing
w=(x,v)
with
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}
, the field
x
plays the role of the Lagrange field associated with fixing the gradient to
0
, while
v
is the Lagrange parameter associated with fixing the free energy to
Nf
. We bound (
1.18
) from above by substituting the SUSY ansatz values
v=\zeta(\{0\})
and
\langle x,\mathbf{m}\rangle=N\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t
, obtaining an upper bound on the annealed complexity.

This upper bound corresponds to typical TAP states whose empirical distribution is given by the law of
\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,X_{q})
, where
(X_{t})
is the Auffinger–Chen SDE. To obtain the matching lower bound, we verify that this saddle point satisfies the required constraints, using (i) properties of the Auffinger–Chen SDE and (ii) the optimality of
\zeta
in the variational problem.

Section
3
: conditional annealed complexity via a hierarchical skeleton.

We extend the method of Section
2
to compute a
conditional annealed
complexity. We consider an optimizer
\zeta
of the variational problem of Theorem
2
. We fix an
ancestor skeleton
of states
\mathbf{m}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)}
such that
\|\mathbf{m}^{(i)}\|^{2}=Nq_{i}
for every
i\in[n-1]
, and
\langle\mathbf{m}^{(i)}-\mathbf{m}^{(i-1)},\mathbf{m}^{(j)}\rangle=0
for every
i\in\{2,\ldots,n-1\}
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,i-1\}
. We further require that
(\mathbf{m}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)})
has empirical statistics, tested against a specific family of test functions, consistent with the law of
(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q_{1},X_{q_{1}}),\ldots,\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},X_{q_{n-1}}))
. We then compute the annealed complexity of states
\mathbf{m}
lying in the band orthogonal to
\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)}
at free energy
f
,
conditionally
on the skeleton being critical with prescribed free energy, i.e., conditionally on

\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}^{(i)})=0\quad\text{and}\quad F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}^{(i)})=N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),\qquad i=1,\ldots,n-1.

The resulting SUSY ansatz is verified using the same arguments as in Section
2
.

1.6.
Notation

•

For every random variable
X
on
\mathbb{R}
absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue, we denote by
\varphi_{X}
the probability density function of
X
. Given another random variable
Y
on
\mathbb{R}
absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue, we denote by
\varphi_{X\mid Y}(\cdot\mid y)
the probability density function of
X
given
Y=y
.

•

We let
\mathbb{C}^{+}=\{z=x+iy:\ y>0\}
.
Let
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
be compactly supported and set

a:=\inf\mathrm{supp}\nu,\qquad b:=\sup\mathrm{supp}\nu.

We define its Stieltjes transform by

(1.19)

G_{\nu}:\bigl(\mathbb{C}^{+}\cup(\mathbb{R}\setminus[a,b])\bigr)\to\mathbb{C},\qquad z\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu(x)}{z-x}.

We then let
G_{\nu}(a)=\lim_{x\uparrow a}G_{\nu}(x)
and
G_{\nu}(b)=\lim_{x\downarrow b}G_{\nu}(x)
. One can show that
G_{\nu}|_{\mathbb{R}}
is a bijection from
\mathbb{R}\setminus[a,b]
to
(G_{\nu}(a),G_{\nu}(b))\setminus\{0\}
.

•

We denote
[N]
for
\{1,\ldots,N\}
.

•

For every probability measure
\zeta
on
[0,1]
, we let

(1.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{q}^{1}s\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)\zeta([0,s])\mathrm{d}s,

(1.21)

\displaystyle h_{\zeta}(q,m)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{\zeta}^{*}(q,\cdot)(m),

(1.22)

\displaystyle k_{\zeta}(q,m)

\displaystyle=\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,m)+m\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t.

•

We write
\mathbf{m}\in[-1,1]^{N}
for the vector
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{N})
.

•

For
\mathbf{m}\in[-1,1]^{N}
we denote

q_{\mathbf{m}}=\frac{1}{N}\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}.

•

For
\mathbf{m}\in[-1,1]^{N}
we denote

\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{m_{i}}.

•

For every
a<b
, we endow
\mathcal{P}([a,b])
with the metric

(1.23)

\mathrm{dist}(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}):=\int_{a}^{b}\big|\zeta([a,s])-\zeta^{\prime}([a,s])\big|\,\mathrm{d}s,

which corresponds to the
1
-Wasserstein distance on
\mathcal{P}([a,b])
and hence metrizes weak convergence.

We will sometimes abuse notation when

\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([a,b])
and
\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}([a^{\prime},b])
with

a\geq a^{\prime}
by extending
\zeta
to
[a^{\prime},b]
and setting

\mathrm{dist}(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}):=\int_{a}^{b}\bigl|\zeta([a,s])-\zeta^{\prime}([a,s])\bigr|\,\mathrm{d}s.

•

Recall from (
1.8
) and (
1.9
),

\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\zeta)=-\int_{-1}^{1}\Phi_{\zeta}^{*}(q,m)\mathrm{d}\mu(m)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{q}^{1}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t,\quad q:=\int m^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu(m),

\mathrm{TAP}(\mu)=\inf_{\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])}\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\zeta).

•

If
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
, we often abuse notation and write
\zeta([0,t])
for
t\in[q,1]
instead of
\zeta([q,t])
.

•

Recall from Definition
1.1
the TAP free energy at fixed overlap measure,

F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=H(\mathbf{m})-\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})-N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}}),

and the optimal overlap measure
\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}\in\mathcal{P}([q_{\mathbf{m}},1])
, i.e. the unique minimizer in (
1.6
).

•

We write
\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,m)
and
\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}(q,m)
for the first and second partial derivatives of
h_{\zeta}(q,\cdot)
with respect to
m
.

•

For
\mathbf{m}\in[-1,1]^{N}
and
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([0,1])
, we set

Z(\mathbf{m}):=\bigl(\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}),\,F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\bigr)\in\mathbb{R}^{N+1}.

2.
The base annealed computation

In this section, we perform an annealed computation of the TAP free energy on a set of admissible states.

2.1.
Statement of the annealed complexity result

In this subsection, we state the main result of Section
2
: the annealed computation of the TAP complexity. We introduce the SUSY constraint set
\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,\varepsilon)
, which localizes the magnetization vector near a given prefix, and formulate the complexity in terms of the Parisi functional.

Throughout Section
2
, we set

\delta_{\varepsilon}:=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The only properties we use are that
\delta_{\varepsilon}\downarrow 0
and

\varepsilon/\delta_{\varepsilon}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\zeta\in\mathrm{Prefix}_{2}(u,q)
for some
u,q\in(0,1)
.
We define
\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,\varepsilon)
as the set of
\mathbf{m}\in[-1+\delta_{\varepsilon},1-\delta_{\varepsilon}]^{N}
such that

\left|\frac{1}{N}\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}-q\right|\leq\varepsilon,\qquad\left|\frac{1}{N}\langle\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,\mathbf{m}),\mathbf{m}\rangle-\xi^{\prime}(q)\int_{0}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t\right|\leq\varepsilon,

and

\mathrm{dist}\!\bigl(\zeta_{\mathbf{m}},\,\zeta|_{(q,1]}+\zeta([0,q])\,\delta_{q}\bigr)\leq\varepsilon,

where we recall that
\mathrm{dist}
is as in (
1.23
).

Proposition 2.2

(Annealed complexity)
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{R}
,
u\in(0,1)
,
q\in(0,1)
, and
\zeta\in\mathrm{Prefix}_{2}(u,q)
. Recall
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}
from (
1.4
).
Define the
prefix free energy

\mathcal{P}(u;q):=\inf_{\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Prefix}_{2}(u,q)}\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta^{\prime})

and the
complexity functional at level
f

(2.1)

\mathcal{C}_{f}(u;q)\;:=\;u\,\bigl(\mathcal{P}(u;q)-f\bigr).

We assume:

(i)

(Tail optimality.)
The infimum
\mathcal{P}(u;q)
is attained at
\zeta
.

(ii)

(Stationarity.)

(u,q)
is a critical point of
\mathcal{C}_{f}
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and let
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q)
be the number of critical
points of
F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}
in
\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,\varepsilon)
with

\frac{1}{N}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\in(f-\varepsilon,f+\varepsilon)
. Then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q)\bigr]\;=\;\mathcal{C}_{f}(u;q)\;=\;\zeta(\{0\})\,\bigl(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)-f\bigr).

2.2.
Kac–Rice formula

We now develop the Kac–Rice machinery needed to count critical points of the
TAP free energy. The first step is an exact algebraic reduction, special to the
pure
p
-spin case, that expresses the value of
F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}
at a critical
point in terms of a deterministic remainder.

Lemma 2.3

(Pure
p
-spin exact reduction)
.

Assume
\xi(t)=\beta_{p}^{2}t^{p}
for some
p\geq 2
.
For
\mathbf{m}\in(-1,1)^{N}
, let

q_{\mathbf{m}}=\frac{1}{N}\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2},\qquad\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})+N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}}),

and

(2.2)

\mathcal{R}^{\rm ex}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m}):=\frac{1}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},\nabla\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\rangle-\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m}).

Then

F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=\frac{1}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\rangle+\mathcal{R}^{\rm ex}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m}).

In particular, if
\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=0
, then

F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=\mathcal{R}^{\rm ex}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m}).

Proof.

Since
\xi(t)=\beta_{p}^{2}t^{p}
, the Hamiltonian
H
is a homogeneous polynomial
of degree
p
in
\mathbf{m}
. Euler’s identity for homogeneous functions therefore
gives

H(\mathbf{m})=\frac{1}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},\nabla H(\mathbf{m})\rangle,\qquad\mathbf{m}\in(-1,1)^{N}.

Writing
F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}=H-\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}
and inserting the identity above, we
obtain

\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},\nabla H(\mathbf{m})\rangle-\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\rangle+\frac{1}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},\nabla\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\rangle-\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\rangle+\mathcal{R}^{\rm ex}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m}),

where the last step uses the definition (
2.2
).
The second assertion follows by setting

\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=0
.
∎

With this reduction in hand, we turn to the Kac–Rice formulas themselves.
In the mixed case, both the gradient and the free-energy value appear as
conditioning variables in the density, whereas in the pure case
Lemma
2.3
eliminates the need to condition on the
free-energy value, replacing it by a deterministic indicator.

Lemma 2.4

(Kac–Rice: mixed and pure cases)
.

Let
\zeta
be a probability measure on
[0,1]
with a
2
-atom prefix

\{0,q\}
for some
q\in(0,1)
, and let
\varepsilon\in(0,q/2)
.
Let
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q)
be the number of critical points
\mathbf{m}
of

F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}
such that

\mathbf{m}\in\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,\varepsilon)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{1}{N}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\in(f-\varepsilon,f+\varepsilon).

For
\mathbf{m}\in(-1,1)^{N}
, write
q_{\mathbf{m}}:=\frac{1}{N}\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}
.

(i)

(Mixed case.)

Assume that for every
\mathbf{m}\in(-1,1)^{N}
with

q_{\mathbf{m}}\in(q-\varepsilon,q+\varepsilon)
, the Gaussian vector

\bigl(\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}),\,F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\bigr)

is non-degenerate. In particular, this holds for a genuine mixed

p
-spin Hamiltonian, i.e. when

\xi(t)=\sum_{p\geq 2}\beta_{p}^{2}t^{p}

has at least two non-zero coefficients.
Then

(2.3)

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q)]=N\int_{[-1,1]^{N}}\int_{(f-\varepsilon,f+\varepsilon)}\mathbb{E}\!\left[|\det(\nabla^{2}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))|\,\middle|\,\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=0,\,F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=Nf^{\prime}\right]\\
\times\varphi_{(\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}),\,F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))}(0,Nf^{\prime})\,\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,\varepsilon)}(\mathbf{m})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m}\,\mathrm{d}f^{\prime}.

(ii)

(Pure case.)

Assume that
\xi(t)=\beta_{p}^{2}t^{p}
is pure
p
-spin for some
p\geq 2
.
Recalling the notation (
2.2
),

(2.4)

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q)]=\int_{[-1,1]^{N}}\mathbb{E}\!\left[|\det(\nabla^{2}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))|\,\middle|\,\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=0\right]\varphi_{\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})}(0)\\
\times\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,\varepsilon)}(\mathbf{m})\,\mathbf{1}_{(f-\varepsilon,f+\varepsilon)}\!\left(\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{R}^{\rm ex}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m}.

Proof.

Step 1: reduction to the interior.

Set

U:=(-1,1)^{N},\qquad U_{q,\varepsilon}:=\left\{\mathbf{m}\in U:\left|\frac{1}{N}\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}-q\right|<\varepsilon\right\}.

Since
\varepsilon<q/2
, every
\mathbf{m}\in U_{q,\varepsilon}
satisfies

q_{\mathbf{m}}>q/2>0
. By definition of
\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,\varepsilon)
,
every critical point counted by
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q)
belongs to
U_{q,\varepsilon}
.

We first verify that no critical points lie on the boundary

\partial([-1,1]^{N})
. Recall that

F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=H(\mathbf{m})-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Phi_{\zeta}^{*}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})-N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}}),\qquad q_{\mathbf{m}}=\frac{1}{N}\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}.

Fix
r\in[0,1]
. Since
x\mapsto\Phi_{\zeta}(r,x)
is strictly convex,
the map
x\mapsto\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(r,x)
is strictly increasing with
range
(-1,1)
, and therefore

\partial_{m}\Phi_{\zeta}^{*}(r,m)=(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(r,\cdot))^{-1}(m),\qquad m\in(-1,1).

In particular,

\lim_{m\uparrow 1}\partial_{m}\Phi_{\zeta}^{*}(r,m)=+\infty,\qquad\lim_{m\downarrow-1}\partial_{m}\Phi_{\zeta}^{*}(r,m)=-\infty.

Hence
F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}
is
C^{2}
on
U
, while if some coordinate

m_{i}=\pm 1
, then the partial derivative
\partial_{i}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})

diverges because of the term
\partial_{m}\Phi_{\zeta}^{*}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})
.
Thus there are no critical points on
\partial([-1,1]^{N})
, and the critical
points of
F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}
in
[-1,1]^{N}
coincide with those in
U
.

Step 2: mixed case.

Assume (i). Since the TAP correction is deterministic, the covariance
of

\bigl(\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}),F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\bigr)

coincides with that of
\bigl(\nabla H(\mathbf{m}),H(\mathbf{m})\bigr)
.
For
\mathbf{m}\in U_{q,\varepsilon}
and
r:=q_{\mathbf{m}}
, the covariance identities read

\mathrm{Cov}(\partial_{i}H(\mathbf{m}),\partial_{j}H(\mathbf{m}))=\xi^{\prime}(r)\delta_{ij}+\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(r)}{N}m_{i}m_{j},

\mathrm{Cov}(\partial_{i}H(\mathbf{m}),H(\mathbf{m}))=\xi^{\prime}(r)m_{i},\qquad\mathrm{Var}(H(\mathbf{m}))=N\xi(r).

Hence for every
(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}
,

(2.5)

\mathrm{Var}\!\bigl(\langle x,\nabla H(\mathbf{m})\rangle+vH(\mathbf{m})\bigr)=\xi^{\prime}(r)\|x\|^{2}+\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(r)}{N}\langle\mathbf{m},x\rangle^{2}+2v\xi^{\prime}(r)\langle\mathbf{m},x\rangle+N\xi(r)v^{2}.

If
\xi(t)=\sum_{p\geq 2}\beta_{p}^{2}t^{p}
has at least two non-zero coefficients,
write
x=x_{\perp}+a\mathbf{m}
with
\langle x_{\perp},\mathbf{m}\rangle=0
. Then

\eqref{eq:var-mixed-KR}=\xi^{\prime}(r)\|x_{\perp}\|^{2}+N\begin{pmatrix}a&v\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}r(\xi^{\prime}(r)+r\xi^{\prime\prime}(r))&r\xi^{\prime}(r)\\
r\xi^{\prime}(r)&\xi(r)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a\\
v\end{pmatrix}.

The determinant of the
2\times 2
matrix equals

\displaystyle\det\begin{pmatrix}r(\xi^{\prime}(r)+r\xi^{\prime\prime}(r))&r\xi^{\prime}(r)\\
r\xi^{\prime}(r)&\xi(r)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{p\geq 2}\beta_{p}^{2}r^{p}\right)\left(\sum_{p\geq 2}p^{2}\beta_{p}^{2}r^{p}\right)-\left(\sum_{p\geq 2}p\beta_{p}^{2}r^{p}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{p,\ell\geq 2}\beta_{p}^{2}\beta_{\ell}^{2}(p-\ell)^{2}r^{p+\ell}>0,

so the quadratic form is positive for every
(x,v)\neq(0,0)
.
The non-degeneracy hypothesis in (i) is therefore satisfied in the
mixed
p
-spin case.

We now apply the Kac–Rice formula (e.g.
[
1
]
,
Corollaries 11.3.2 and 11.3.5) to the
(N+1)
-dimensional Gaussian field

\mathbf{m}\mapsto\bigl(\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}),F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\bigr)

on the open set
U_{q,\varepsilon}
, restricted to the Borel set

\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,\varepsilon)\cap U_{q,\varepsilon}
and the level window

F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\in(N(f-\varepsilon),N(f+\varepsilon))
. This gives

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q)]=\int_{U_{q,\varepsilon}}\int_{(N(f-\varepsilon),N(f+\varepsilon))}\mathbb{E}\!\left[|\det(\nabla^{2}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))|\,\middle|\,\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=0,\,F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=t\right]\\
\times\varphi_{(\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}),\,F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))}(0,t)\,\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,\varepsilon)}(\mathbf{m})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m}\,\mathrm{d}t.

The change of variables
t=Nf^{\prime}
yields
\mathrm{d}t=N\,\mathrm{d}f^{\prime}
and therefore

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q)]=N\int_{U_{q,\varepsilon}}\int_{(f-\varepsilon,f+\varepsilon)}\mathbb{E}\!\left[|\det(\nabla^{2}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))|\,\middle|\,\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=0,\,F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=Nf^{\prime}\right]\\
\times\varphi_{(\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}),F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))}(0,Nf^{\prime})\,\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,\varepsilon)}(\mathbf{m})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m}\,\mathrm{d}f^{\prime}.

Finally, since
\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,\varepsilon)}(\mathbf{m})=0
outside
U_{q,\varepsilon}
and

[-1,1]^{N}\setminus U
has Lebesgue measure zero, we may extend the outer
integral to
[-1,1]^{N}
, which gives (
2.3
).

Step 3: pure case.

Assume now (ii) and set
Y(\mathbf{m}):=\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})
for

\mathbf{m}\in U
. By Lemma
2.3
,

(2.6)

Y(\mathbf{m})=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=\mathcal{R}^{\rm ex}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m}).

Therefore

\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q)=\#\left\{\mathbf{m}\in U_{q,\varepsilon}:Y(\mathbf{m})=0,\,\mathbf{m}\in\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,\varepsilon),\,\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{R}^{\rm ex}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\in(f-\varepsilon,f+\varepsilon)\right\}.

For each fixed
\mathbf{m}\in U_{q,\varepsilon}
, the vector
Y(\mathbf{m})
is Gaussian with
covariance

\mathrm{Cov}(Y(\mathbf{m}))=\mathrm{Cov}(\nabla H(\mathbf{m}))=\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\mathrm{Id}+\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})}{N}\mathbf{m}\mathbf{m}^{\top}.

Since
q_{\mathbf{m}}>q/2>0
and
\xi^{\prime}(0)=0<\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})
by strict convexity
of
\xi
, this covariance matrix is positive definite. Hence
Y(\mathbf{m})
is
non-degenerate for every
\mathbf{m}\in U_{q,\varepsilon}
.

We may therefore apply the Kac–Rice formula to the
N
-dimensional Gaussian
field
Y
on the Borel set

A_{\zeta,\varepsilon,f}:=\left\{\mathbf{m}\in U_{q,\varepsilon}:\mathbf{m}\in\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,\varepsilon),\,\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{R}^{\rm ex}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\in(f-\varepsilon,f+\varepsilon)\right\}.

Since
\nabla Y(\mathbf{m})=\nabla^{2}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})
, this yields

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q)]=\int_{U_{q,\varepsilon}}\mathbb{E}\!\left[|\det(\nabla^{2}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))|\,\middle|\,\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=0\right]\varphi_{\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})}(0)\\
\times\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,\varepsilon)}(\mathbf{m})\,\mathbf{1}_{(f-\varepsilon,f+\varepsilon)}\!\left(\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{R}^{\rm ex}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m}.

As before,
\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,\varepsilon)}
vanishes outside
U_{q,\varepsilon}
and

[-1,1]^{N}\setminus U
has Lebesgue measure zero, so we may extend the integral
to
[-1,1]^{N}
, giving (
2.4
).
∎

2.3.
Reduction from the Ward identities

In the Kac–Rice formula (
2.3
), we must evaluate the joint density of

(\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}),\,F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))
at
(0,Nf)
. Leveraging the SUSY
heuristics from Section
A.1
, we derive a particularly simple representation in the next lemma.

Lemma 2.5
.

Assume that
\xi
is a genuine mixture, i.e.
\xi(t)=\sum_{p}\beta_{p}^{2}t^{p}
with
at least two nonzero coefficients. In particular, the quantity

(2.7)

D(q):=\xi(q)\bigl(\xi^{\prime}(q)+q\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\bigr)-q\,\xi^{\prime}(q)^{2}=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{a,b}\beta_{a}^{2}\beta_{b}^{2}(a-b)^{2}\,q^{a+b-1}

is strictly positive on
(0,1)
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
,
u\in(0,1)
,
q\in(0,1)
and
f\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\zeta\in\mathrm{Prefix}_{2}(u,q)
where
\mathrm{Prefix}_{2}
is as in
Definition
1.2
.

Let
\delta\in[\varepsilon,1)
and
\mathbf{m}\in[-1+\delta,1-\delta]^{N}
and denote

Z(\mathbf{m}):=\bigl(\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}),\,F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\bigr)
. Suppose in addition that
q_{\mathbf{m}}\in[q-\varepsilon,q+\varepsilon]
and

\mathrm{dist}\!\bigl(\zeta_{\mathbf{m}},\,\zeta|_{(q,1]}+\zeta([0,q])\,\delta_{q}\bigr)\leq\varepsilon
,
where both measures are viewed as elements of
\mathcal{P}([0,1])
and
\mathrm{dist}

is the metric (
1.23
).

(i)

The log-density of
Z(\mathbf{m})
at
(0,Nf)
satisfies

(2.8)

\log\varphi_{Z(\mathbf{m})}(0,Nf)\\
\leq\frac{1}{2}N\xi(q)u^{2}-\frac{1}{2}N\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\left(\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{2\xi^{\prime}(q)}\|\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,\mathbf{m})-u\xi^{\prime}(q)\mathbf{m}\|^{2}\\
-\frac{N}{2}\log(2\pi\xi^{\prime}(q))+\frac{N}{2\xi^{\prime}(q)q}\left(\frac{1}{N}\langle\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,\mathbf{m}),\mathbf{m}\rangle-\xi^{\prime}(q)\int_{0}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t\right)^{2}\\
-u\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})+N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q)+Nf\right)+O\!\left(N\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\right)+O(\log N),

where the implicit constant depends only on
\xi
,
q
, and
u
.

(ii)

Suppose in addition that

(2.9)

\langle\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,\mathbf{m}),\mathbf{m}\rangle=N\xi^{\prime}(q)\int_{0}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t+O(N\varepsilon)

and

(2.10)

\sum_{i=1}^{N}(h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})-\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})m_{i})+N\int_{0}^{q}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{2}N\int_{q}^{1}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t=-Nf+O(N\varepsilon).

Then,

(2.11)

\log\varphi_{Z(\mathbf{m})}(0,Nf)\\
=\frac{1}{2}N\xi(q)u^{2}-\frac{1}{2}N\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\left(\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{2\xi^{\prime}(q)}\|\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,\mathbf{m})-u\xi^{\prime}(q)\mathbf{m}\|^{2}-\frac{N}{2}\log(2\pi\xi^{\prime}(q))\\
-u\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})+N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q)+Nf\right)+O\!\left(N\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\right)+O(\log N).

Proof.

Throughout we set
q_{\mathbf{m}}:=\frac{1}{N}\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}
.

Step 1: exact Gaussian representation at
q_{\mathbf{m}}

By definition (
1.6
),

F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=H(\mathbf{m})-\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})-N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}}).

For the gradient, the exact computation in the proof of Lemma
B.8

(see (
B.39
) and (
B.40
)) gives, for every
i\in[N]
,

(2.12)

\partial_{i}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=\partial_{i}H(\mathbf{m})-k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})+\Delta_{i}(\mathbf{m},\zeta),

where
\Delta_{i}
is controlled by (
B.35
).
Indeed the comparison measure

\zeta|_{(q_{\mathbf{m}},1]}+\zeta([0,q_{\mathbf{m}}])\delta_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}
has an atom of size

\zeta([0,q_{\mathbf{m}}])\geq u
at
q_{\mathbf{m}}
, so

|\Delta_{i}|\leq\frac{C}{\delta}\,\mathrm{dist}\!\bigl(\zeta_{\mathbf{m}},\,\zeta|_{(q_{\mathbf{m}},1]}+\zeta([0,q_{\mathbf{m}}])\delta_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}\bigr).

Moreover, the
\mathrm{dist}
-distance between the measures

\zeta|_{(q_{\mathbf{m}},1]}+\zeta([0,q_{\mathbf{m}}])\delta_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}
and

\zeta|_{(q,1]}+\zeta([0,q])\delta_{q}
is at most
C\varepsilon
; together with the
hypothesis

\mathrm{dist}(\zeta_{\mathbf{m}},\,\zeta|_{(q,1]}+\zeta([0,q])\delta_{q})<\varepsilon
and the triangle
inequality, this yields

|\Delta_{i}|\leq C^{\prime}\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}

componentwise.

Write
\Delta:=(\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{N})
and define

z_{\mathbf{m}}:=\begin{pmatrix}k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})-\Delta\\[3.0pt]
Nf+\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})+N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\end{pmatrix},\qquad\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}:=\operatorname{Cov}\bigl(Z(\mathbf{m})\bigr).

Then
Z(\mathbf{m})
is Gaussian with mean
-z_{\mathbf{m}}
and covariance
\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}
, so

(2.13)

\log\varphi_{Z(\mathbf{m})}(0,Nf)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle z_{\mathbf{m}},\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}\,z_{\mathbf{m}}\rangle-\tfrac{1}{2}\log\det(2\pi\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}).

Step 2: block structure of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}

The covariance
\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}
admits the
(N\!+\!1)\times(N\!+\!1)
block decomposition

\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}=\begin{pmatrix}A_{\mathbf{m}}&b_{\mathbf{m}}\\
b_{\mathbf{m}}^{\top}&c_{\mathbf{m}}\end{pmatrix},\quad A_{\mathbf{m}}=\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})I_{N}+\tfrac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})}{N}\mathbf{m}\mathbf{m}^{\top},\quad b_{\mathbf{m}}=\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\mathbf{m},\quad c_{\mathbf{m}}=N\xi(q_{\mathbf{m}}).

By the matrix determinant lemma,

\det A_{\mathbf{m}}=\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})^{N-1}\bigl(\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})+q_{\mathbf{m}}\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\bigr)
,
and the Schur complement

S_{\mathbf{m}}:=c_{\mathbf{m}}-b_{\mathbf{m}}^{\top}A_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}b_{\mathbf{m}}=\frac{N\,D(q_{\mathbf{m}})}{\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})+q_{\mathbf{m}}\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})}

is strictly positive since
\xi
is a mixture. Hence

(2.14)

\log\det(\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}})=(N\!-\!1)\log\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})+\log\bigl(\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})+q_{\mathbf{m}}\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\bigr)+\log\frac{N\,D(q_{\mathbf{m}})}{\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})+q_{\mathbf{m}}\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})}.

Since
|q_{\mathbf{m}}-q|\leq\varepsilon
and all functions of
q
appearing above are smooth on
(0,1)
,

(2.15)

\log\det(\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}})=N\log\xi^{\prime}(q)+O_{\varepsilon}(N)+O(\log N).

For the inverse, the standard block-inversion formula gives

(2.16)

\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}A_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}+\frac{1}{S_{\mathbf{m}}}A_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}b_{\mathbf{m}}b_{\mathbf{m}}^{\top}A_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}&-\frac{1}{S_{\mathbf{m}}}A_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}b_{\mathbf{m}}\\[4.0pt]
-\frac{1}{S_{\mathbf{m}}}b_{\mathbf{m}}^{\top}A_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}&\frac{1}{S_{\mathbf{m}}}\end{pmatrix},

so the gradient–gradient block of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}
is
O(1)
,
the scalar entry is
O(1/N)
, and the cross entries are
O(1/N)
.

Step 3: upper bound
By the convex-dual identity

-\langle z_{\mathbf{m}},\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}z_{\mathbf{m}}\rangle=\inf_{w}\bigl\{\langle w,\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}w\rangle-2\langle w,z_{\mathbf{m}}\rangle\bigr\},

we bound the right-hand side from above by evaluating at a test point

w=(x,u)
with the constraint

\langle\mathbf{m},x\rangle=N\!\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t
.

Since
k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m)=\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m)+m\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\int_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t

and
\int_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t=\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t+O(\varepsilon)
, the

\xi^{\prime\prime}
-terms in the quadratic form and the linear form cancel up to

O(N\varepsilon/\delta)
on the constraint set.
The defect
\Delta
contributes

2|\langle x,\Delta\rangle|\leq C\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\bigl(\|x\|^{2}+N\bigr)
,
which is controlled by the positive
\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\|x\|^{2}
term and an additive

O(N\varepsilon/\delta)
.
After these cancellations,

(2.17)

-\langle z_{\mathbf{m}},\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}z_{\mathbf{m}}\rangle\leq N\xi(q_{\mathbf{m}})u^{2}-N\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\!\left(\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\!2}+O\!\left(N\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\right)\\
+\inf_{x:\langle x,\mathbf{m}\rangle=N\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t}\Bigl(\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\|x\|^{2}-2\langle\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})-u\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\mathbf{m},\,x\rangle\Bigr)\\
-2u\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})+N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}})+Nf\bigr).

Optimising the constrained infimum over
x
by Lagrange duality
(using
\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}=Nq_{\mathbf{m}}
and

uq_{\mathbf{m}}+\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t+O(\varepsilon)
)
and then optimising the concave quadratic in the multiplier
\lambda
,

(2.18)

\inf_{x:\langle x,\mathbf{m}\rangle=N\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t}\Bigl(\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\|x\|^{2}-2\bigl\langle\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})-u\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\mathbf{m},\,x\bigr\rangle\Bigr)\\
=-\frac{\|\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})-u\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\mathbf{m}\|^{2}}{\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})}+\frac{N}{\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})q_{\mathbf{m}}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{N}\bigl\langle\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m}),\mathbf{m}\bigr\rangle-\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\!\int_{0}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t\Bigr)^{2}+O_{\varepsilon}(N).

All quantities in (
2.17
)–(
2.18
) involve
q_{\mathbf{m}}
rather than
q
.
Since
|q_{\mathbf{m}}-q|\leq\varepsilon
and the functions
h_{\zeta}(\cdot,m)
,
\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}
,
\xi^{\prime}
,

\xi^{\prime\prime}
,
\xi
are smooth in their overlap argument, replacing each occurrence of
q_{\mathbf{m}}
by

q
introduces errors that are
O(\varepsilon/\delta)
pointwise in
m\in[-1+\delta,1-\delta]
and
O(N\varepsilon/\delta)
when summed over
i
. In the positive-square term, the
cross-contribution from the
O(\varepsilon)
shift only inflates the upper bound and is therefore
harmless. Together with (
2.15
) and (
2.13
),
this yields (
2.8
) with overall remainder

O(N\varepsilon/\delta)+O(\log N)
.

Step 4: lower bound

Suppose that (
2.9
) and (
2.10
) hold. Set

x:=\frac{1}{\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})}\bigl(\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})-u\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\mathbf{m}\bigr),\qquad w:=(x,u)\in\mathbb{R}^{N+1}.

Since the quadratic
w^{\prime}\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\langle w^{\prime},\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}w^{\prime}\rangle-\langle w^{\prime},z_{\mathbf{m}}\rangle

is strictly convex, defining
r:=\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}w-z_{\mathbf{m}}
we have

(2.19)

\tfrac{1}{2}\langle w,\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}w\rangle-\langle w,z_{\mathbf{m}}\rangle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle z_{\mathbf{m}},\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}z_{\mathbf{m}}\rangle+\tfrac{1}{2}\langle r,\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}r\rangle.

For the gradient block of
r
, a direct computation gives

\langle\mathbf{m},x\rangle=\frac{1}{\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})}\bigl(\langle\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m}),\mathbf{m}\rangle-u\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})Nq_{\mathbf{m}}\bigr).

By (
2.9
) and

\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})=\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})+O(\varepsilon)
,
we get
\langle\mathbf{m},x\rangle=N\int_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t+O(N\varepsilon)
.
Using the definition of
k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\cdot)
and the defect bound

\|\Delta\|_{\infty}\leq C^{\prime}\varepsilon/\delta
from Step 1, this gives

(2.20)

[r]_{\mathrm{grad}}=O(\varepsilon/\delta)\text{ componentwise}.

For the scalar block of
r
, using

\langle\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m}),\mathbf{m}\rangle=\sum_{i}\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})m_{i}

together with (
2.9
) and
\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\cdot)=\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,\cdot)+O(\varepsilon)
,

\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})-\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})m_{i}\bigr)+N\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\Bigl(uq_{\mathbf{m}}+\int_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t\Bigr)+O(N\varepsilon).

Combined with
u\bigl(\xi(q_{\mathbf{m}})-q_{\mathbf{m}}\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\bigr)=-\int_{0}^{q_{\mathbf{m}}}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t

(which holds since
\zeta([0,t])=u
for
t\in[0,q)
),

-\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})+\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\langle\mathbf{m},x\rangle+Nu\xi(q_{\mathbf{m}})-N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\\
=-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})-\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})m_{i}\bigr)-N\!\int_{0}^{q_{\mathbf{m}}}\!t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t-\frac{N}{2}\!\int_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}^{1}\!t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t+O(N\varepsilon).

Replacing
q_{\mathbf{m}}
by
q
on the right-hand side costs
O(N\varepsilon)
,
and by (
2.10
) the resulting expression equals
Nf+O(N\varepsilon)
.
Since
[z_{\mathbf{m}}]_{N+1}=Nf+\sum_{i}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})+N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}})
,

(2.21)

[r]_{N+1}=O(N\varepsilon).

By (
2.20
), (
2.21
), and the blockwise scaling of

\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}
from (
2.16
),

\langle r,\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}r\rangle\leq C\bigl(\|r_{\mathrm{grad}}\|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{N}|r_{N+1}|^{2}\bigr)=O\!\left(N\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\right)+O(N\varepsilon^{2})=O\!\left(N\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\right).

Evaluating
\frac{1}{2}\langle w,\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}w\rangle-\langle w,z_{\mathbf{m}}\rangle

at
w=(x,u)
by the same algebra as in Step 3 (the constrained optimisation in (
2.18
)
is now nearly attained since

\langle\mathbf{m},x\rangle=N\int_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t+O(N\varepsilon)
),
and replacing
q_{\mathbf{m}}
by
q
at the cost of
O(N\varepsilon/\delta)
,

\tfrac{1}{2}\langle w,\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}w\rangle-\langle w,z_{\mathbf{m}}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}N\xi(q)u^{2}-\frac{1}{2}N\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\Bigl(\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t\Bigr)^{\!2}-\frac{1}{2\xi^{\prime}(q)}\|\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,\mathbf{m})-u\xi^{\prime}(q)\mathbf{m}\|^{2}\\
-u\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})+N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q)+Nf\Bigr)+O\!\left(N\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\right),

where (
2.9
) ensures that the positive-square term
from (
2.18
) contributes
O(N\varepsilon^{2})=O(N\varepsilon/\delta)
.
Since
\langle r,\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}r\rangle=O(N\varepsilon/\delta)

by (
2.19
),

-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle z_{\mathbf{m}},\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}z_{\mathbf{m}}\rangle=\tfrac{1}{2}\langle w,\Gamma_{\mathbf{m}}w\rangle-\langle w,z_{\mathbf{m}}\rangle+O\!\left(N\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\right).

Together with (
2.13
) and (
2.15
),
this gives (
2.11
).
∎

Let us now discuss the degenerate case.

Lemma 2.6
.

Assume that
\xi
is pure
p
-spin. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Let
u\in(0,1)
,
q\in(0,1)
,
f\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
\zeta\in\mathrm{Prefix}_{2}(u,q)
where
\mathrm{Prefix}_{2}
is as in Definition
1.2
.

Let
\delta\in[\varepsilon,1)
and
\mathbf{m}\in[-1+\delta,1-\delta]^{N}
. Suppose moreover that
q_{\mathbf{m}}\in(q-\varepsilon,q+\varepsilon)
and

\mathrm{dist}\!\bigl(\zeta_{\mathbf{m}},\,\zeta|_{(q,1]}+\zeta([0,q])\,\delta_{q}\bigr)<\varepsilon
.

(i)

The gradient
\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})
is a non-degenerate Gaussian vector in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Its log-density at
0
, tilted by

u\!\left(\frac{1}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},k_{\zeta}(q,\mathbf{m})\rangle-\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})-N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q)-Nf\right),

satisfies

(2.22)

\log\varphi_{\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})}(0)+u\!\left(\frac{1}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},k_{\zeta}(q,\mathbf{m})\rangle-\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})-N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q)-Nf\right)\\
=\frac{1}{2}N\xi(q)u^{2}-\frac{1}{2}N\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\!\left(\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\!2}-\frac{1}{2\xi^{\prime}(q)}\|\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,\mathbf{m})-u\xi^{\prime}(q)\mathbf{m}\|^{2}\\
-\frac{N}{2}\log(2\pi\xi^{\prime}(q))+\frac{N(p-1)}{2p\,\xi^{\prime}(q)\,q}\!\left(\frac{1}{N}\langle\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,\mathbf{m}),\mathbf{m}\rangle-\xi^{\prime}(q)\int_{0}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\!2}\\
-u\!\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})+N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q)+Nf\right)+O\!\left(N\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\right).

(ii)

Suppose moreover that

(2.23)

\langle\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,\mathbf{m}),\mathbf{m}\rangle=N\xi^{\prime}(q)\int_{0}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t+O(N\varepsilon)

and

(2.24)

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})-\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})m_{i}\bigr)+N\int_{0}^{q}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{N}{2}\int_{q}^{1}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t=-Nf+O(N\varepsilon).

Then the squared term in (
2.22
) is
O(N\varepsilon)
, so that

(2.25)

\log\varphi_{\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})}(0)+u\!\left(\frac{1}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},k_{\zeta}(q,\mathbf{m})\rangle-\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})-N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q)-Nf\right)\\
=\frac{1}{2}N\xi(q)u^{2}-\frac{1}{2}N\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\!\left(\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\!2}-\frac{1}{2\xi^{\prime}(q)}\|\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,\mathbf{m})-u\xi^{\prime}(q)\mathbf{m}\|^{2}\\
-\frac{N}{2}\log(2\pi\xi^{\prime}(q))-u\!\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})+N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q)+Nf\right)+O\!\left(N\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\right),

and, on the event
\{\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=0\}
,

(2.26)

F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=Nf+O\!\left(N\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\right).

Proof.

Step 1: reduction to the exact remainder.

By Lemma
2.3
and (
2.2
),

F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=\frac{1}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\rangle+\mathcal{R}^{\rm ex}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m}),\qquad\mathcal{R}^{\rm ex}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=\frac{1}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},\nabla\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\rangle-\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})-N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}}).

Also, by definition,

\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})+N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}}).

Since the distribution functions of

\zeta|_{(q,1]}+\zeta([0,q])\delta_{q}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\zeta|_{(q_{\mathbf{m}},1]}+\zeta([0,q_{\mathbf{m}}])\delta_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}

differ only on the interval between
q
and
q_{\mathbf{m}}
, we have

\mathrm{dist}\!\Bigl(\zeta|_{(q,1]}+\zeta([0,q])\delta_{q},\,\zeta|_{(q_{\mathbf{m}},1]}+\zeta([0,q_{\mathbf{m}}])\delta_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}\Bigr)\leq|q_{\mathbf{m}}-q|=O(\varepsilon).

Hence

\mathrm{dist}\!\Bigl(\zeta_{\mathbf{m}},\,\zeta|_{(q_{\mathbf{m}},1]}+\zeta([0,q_{\mathbf{m}}])\delta_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}\Bigr)=O(\varepsilon).

Applying Lemma
B.8
, (
B.35
), to the
measure
\zeta|_{(q_{\mathbf{m}},1]}+\zeta([0,q_{\mathbf{m}}])\delta_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}
, and using that
this measure agrees with
\zeta
above
q_{\mathbf{m}}
, we get

\nabla\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})+O(\varepsilon/\delta),

where the error is understood componentwise. Since
|m_{i}|\leq 1
for every
i
,
this yields

(2.27)

F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=\frac{1}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\rangle+\frac{1}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})\rangle-\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})-N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}})+O\!\left(N\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\right).

In particular, on the event
\{\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=0\}
,

(2.28)

F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=\frac{1}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})\rangle-\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})-N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}})+O\!\left(N\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\right).

Step 2: density of the gradient.

Set

G_{\mathbf{m}}:=\mathrm{Cov}(\nabla H(\mathbf{m}))=\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\mathrm{Id}+\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})}{N}\mathbf{m}\mathbf{m}^{\top}.

Since
\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}=Nq_{\mathbf{m}}
and
\xi
is pure
p
-spin, the identities

q_{\mathbf{m}}\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})=(p-1)\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})
and

p\,\xi(q_{\mathbf{m}})=q_{\mathbf{m}}\,\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})
hold. Therefore
G_{\mathbf{m}}
has
eigenvalue
\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})
on
\mathbf{m}^{\perp}
and eigenvalue

p\,\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})
on
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{m}
, so that

(2.29)

G_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})}\mathrm{Id}-\frac{p-1}{p\,\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\,q_{\mathbf{m}}}\,\frac{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{m}^{\top}}{N},\qquad\log\det(2\pi G_{\mathbf{m}})=N\log(2\pi\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}}))+O(1).

By Step 1,
\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})
is Gaussian with covariance
G_{\mathbf{m}}

and mean
-k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})+e
where
\|e\|_{\infty}\leq C\varepsilon/\delta
.
Since
\|G_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1/\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})
and
\|k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})\|^{2}=O(N)
, the cross-term

\langle k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m}),G_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}e\rangle
is
O(N\varepsilon/\delta)
. Hence

(2.30)

\log\varphi_{\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})}(0)=-\frac{1}{2}\langle k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m}),G_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\log\det(2\pi G_{\mathbf{m}})+O\!\left(N\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\right).

Step 3: completing the square.

By (
2.28
), the tilt differs from

\frac{u}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})\rangle
only by the deterministic
term

-u\!\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})+N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}})+Nf\right).

Thus we must compute

-\frac{1}{2}\langle k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m}),G_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})\rangle+\frac{u}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})\rangle.

Recalling that

k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})=\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})+\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\!\left(\int_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t\right)\mathbf{m}

and using (
2.29
), together with

q_{\mathbf{m}}\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})=(p-1)\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})
and

p\,\xi(q_{\mathbf{m}})=q_{\mathbf{m}}\,\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})
, a direct computation gives

(2.31)

-\frac{1}{2}\langle k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m}),G_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})\rangle+\frac{u}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})\rangle\\
=\frac{1}{2}N\xi(q_{\mathbf{m}})u^{2}-\frac{1}{2}N\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\!\left(\int_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\!2}-\frac{1}{2\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})}\|\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})-u\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\mathbf{m}\|^{2}\\
+\frac{N(p-1)}{2p\,\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\,q_{\mathbf{m}}}\left(\frac{1}{N}\langle\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m}),\mathbf{m}\rangle-\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\!\left(uq_{\mathbf{m}}+\int_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t\right)\right)^{\!2}.

Substituting (
2.31
) into (
2.30
), and then replacing
q_{\mathbf{m}}
by
q
at cost
O(N\varepsilon/\delta)
, proves (
2.22
).

Step 4: critical value.

From (
2.28
), at a critical point we have

F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=\frac{1}{p}\langle\mathbf{m},k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})\rangle-\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})-N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}})+O\!\left(N\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\right).

Using

k_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})=\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m})+\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\!\left(\int_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t\right)\mathbf{m}

and
q_{\mathbf{m}}\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})=(p-1)\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})
, we get

(2.32)

F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})-\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},m_{i})m_{i}\bigr)\\
+\frac{p-1}{p}\!\left(N\xi^{\prime}(q_{\mathbf{m}})\int_{q_{\mathbf{m}}}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t-\langle\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{m}),\mathbf{m}\rangle\right)-N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q_{\mathbf{m}})+O\!\left(N\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\right).

Using
|q_{\mathbf{m}}-q|\leq\varepsilon
, the smoothness of
q\mapsto\xi(q),\xi^{\prime}(q),\xi^{\prime\prime}(q),\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q)
,
and the fact that
m\mapsto h_{\zeta}(q,m)
and
m\mapsto\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,m)
vary by
O(\varepsilon/\delta)

when
q_{\mathbf{m}}
is replaced by
q
(uniformly for
|m|\leq 1-\delta
), the assumptions (
2.23
)–(
2.24
)
imply the corresponding
q_{\mathbf{m}}
-versions up to
O(N\varepsilon/\delta)
. Therefore the square term in
(
2.31
) is
O(N\varepsilon/\delta)
, which gives (
2.25
);
inserting the same replacements into (
2.32
) yields
(
2.26
) with error
O(N\varepsilon/\delta)
.
∎

2.4.
Determinant asymptotics

We compute the expected value of the determinant appearing in the Kac–Rice formula (
2.3
). By definition,
\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}
minimizes
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})},\cdot)
, and it has an atom at
q_{\mathbf{m}}
. Consequently,
q_{\mathbf{m}}
is an optimizer of a certain auxiliary function. We show that the first- and second-order optimality conditions for this nonconvex problem then determine the value of the subordination function, and hence that of the determinant of the free convolution.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\zeta
be a probability measure on
[0,1]
with prefix
\{0,q\}
with
q\in(0,1)
. Let
f\in\mathbb{R}
. There exists
C_{\varepsilon}>0
, depending only on
\varepsilon
,
f
, and
\xi
, with
C_{\varepsilon}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, such that the following holds uniformly over
\mathbf{m}\in\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,\varepsilon)
: Letting
\mathcal{B}:=\{\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=0,\,F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=Nf\}
,

\left|\log\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\det\nabla^{2}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})|\mid\mathcal{B}\bigr]-\log\prod_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})-\frac{N}{2}\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\left(\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\!2}\right|\leq C_{\varepsilon}N.

Proof.

Let
q_{\mathbf{m}}=\frac{1}{N}\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}\in(q-\varepsilon,q+\varepsilon)
. For a probability measure
\zeta^{\prime}
on
[q^{\prime},1]
, set

T_{\zeta^{\prime}}:m\in[-1,1]\mapsto\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta^{\prime}}(q^{\prime},m)+\xi^{\prime\prime}(q^{\prime})\int_{q^{\prime}}^{1}\zeta^{\prime}([0,t])\mathrm{d}t.

By Lemma
C.4
,

(2.33)

\log\mathbb{E}[|\det(\nabla^{2}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))|\mid\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=0,F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=Nf]\\
