, and the tail optimality assumption gives
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\big|_{\theta=0^{+}}\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta_{\theta})\geq 0
. By the first-variation formula from Lemma
B.6
(ii)
, this yields (
3.32
) exactly as in the proof of (
2.51
).

For the stationarity conditions, differentiating
\mathcal{C}_{f}^{(n)}(\bm{u};\bm{q})
with respect to
u_{l}
for
l\in[n-1]
gives
\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{l}}\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)=0
; combined with the first-variation formula in
u_{l}
, this yields (
3.29
) by the same computation as for (
2.49
). Differentiating with respect to
u_{n}
gives
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)+u_{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{n}}\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)=f
, which together with the first-variation formula yields (
3.30
). Finally, differentiating with respect to
q_{k}
and using the stationarity condition
\partial_{q_{k}}\mathcal{C}_{f}^{(n)}=0
gives (
3.31
), by the same argument as for (
2.50
).
∎

3.4.
Proof of the conditional annealed estimate

We are now in a position to prove the section’s main result, following the proof of Proposition
2.2
.

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

Set

u_{n}:=\zeta([0,q_{n})),\qquad\zeta_{\star}:=\zeta|_{(q_{n},1]}+\zeta([0,q_{n}])\delta_{q_{n}}.

As in the proof of Proposition
2.2
, we write
C_{\varepsilon}
for a
positive quantity such that
C_{\varepsilon}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, and
O_{\varepsilon}(N)
for any
quantity bounded in absolute value by
C_{\varepsilon}N
.

Step 1: upper bound.

By Lemma
2.4
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q_{n},\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)})\mid\mathrm{Skel}_{n-1}=(0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),\ldots,0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta))\Bigr]

\displaystyle\qquad=N\int_{[-1,1]^{N}}\int_{f-\varepsilon}^{f+\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[|\det(\nabla^{2}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))|\,\Big|\,\mathrm{Skel}_{n}=(0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),\ldots,0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),0,Nf^{\prime})\Bigr]

\displaystyle\hskip 80.00012pt\times\varphi_{Z(\mathbf{m})\mid\mathrm{Skel}_{n-1}}\Bigl(0,Nf^{\prime}\,\Big|\,0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),\ldots,0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 80.00012pt\times\mathbf{1}_{(\mathbf{m}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)},\mathbf{m})\in\mathrm{SUSY}_{n}(\zeta,\varepsilon)}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m}\,\mathrm{d}f^{\prime}.

For every
\mathbf{m}
in the domain of integration, the conditional law of
\nabla^{2}H(\mathbf{m})
given
\mathrm{Skel}_{n}
differs from its law given
(Z(\mathbf{m}))
by a finite-rank (at most
2(n-1)
-rank) perturbation arising from the conditioning on
\mathrm{Skel}_{n-1}
. Since Lemma
C.4
computes the determinant via the free convolution of
T_{\zeta}\#\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})}
with a semicircle law, and a finite-rank perturbation changes the Stieltjes transform by
O(1/N)
, the same asymptotic holds. More precisely, computations analogous to those in the proof of Lemma
2.7
give

\log\mathbb{E}\Bigl[|\det(\nabla^{2}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))|\,\Big|\,\mathrm{Skel}_{n}=(0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),\ldots,0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),0,Nf^{\prime})\Bigr]\\
=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m_{i})+\frac{N}{2}\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{n})\Bigl(\int_{q_{n}}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t\Bigr)^{2}+O_{\varepsilon}(N).

Next, for every
k\leq n-1
, (
3.29
) and (
3.10
) give

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{k},m_{i}^{(k)})-\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{k},m_{i}^{(k)})\,m_{i}^{(k)}\bigr)+\frac{N}{2}\int_{0}^{1}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t\\
+\frac{N}{2}\int_{0}^{q_{k}}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t=-N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)+O_{\varepsilon}(N).

Hence the hypothesis (
3.14
) of Lemma
3.4

holds with
f_{1}=\cdots=f_{n-1}=\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)
, and therefore

\displaystyle\log\varphi_{Z(\mathbf{m})\mid\mathrm{Skel}_{n-1}}\Bigl(0,Nf^{\prime}\,\Big|\,0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),\ldots,0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\leq-\frac{N}{2}\xi^{\prime\prime}(q_{n})\Bigl(\int_{q_{n}}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\bigl\|\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},\mathbf{m})-\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)})\bigr\|^{2}}{2(\xi^{\prime}(q_{n})-\xi^{\prime}(q_{n-1}))}

\displaystyle\qquad-u_{n}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m_{i})-m_{i}\,\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m_{i})\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad+u_{n}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},m_{i}^{(n-1)})-m_{i}^{(n-1)}\,\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},m_{i}^{(n-1)})\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad-Nu_{n}(f-\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta))-\frac{N}{2}\log\bigl(2\pi(\xi^{\prime}(q_{n})-\xi^{\prime}(q_{n-1}))\bigr)+O_{\varepsilon}(N),

where we used
|f^{\prime}-f|\leq\varepsilon
and absorbed the resulting error into
O_{\varepsilon}(N)
.

Combining the last two displays and integrating coordinatewise, we obtain

\displaystyle\log\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q_{n},\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)})\mid\mathrm{Skel}_{n-1}=(0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),\ldots,0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta))\Bigr]

\displaystyle\quad\leq u_{n}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},m_{i}^{(n-1)})-m_{i}^{(n-1)}\,\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},m_{i}^{(n-1)})\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{N}{2}\log\bigl(2\pi(\xi^{\prime}(q_{n})-\xi^{\prime}(q_{n-1}))\bigr)+Nu_{n}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)-f)+O_{\varepsilon}(N)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log\int_{-1}^{1}\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)\,\exp\!\biggl(-\frac{(\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)-\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},m_{i}^{(n-1)}))^{2}}{2(\xi^{\prime}(q_{n})-\xi^{\prime}(q_{n-1}))}

\displaystyle\hskip 140.00021pt-u_{n}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)-m\,\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)\bigr)\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}m.

By (
2.45
),

h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},a)-a\,\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},a)=-\Phi_{\zeta}\bigl(q_{n-1},\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},a)\bigr),

and the change of variables
y=\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)
together with
Lemma
B.3
gives

\int_{-1}^{1}\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)\,\exp\!\biggl(-\frac{(\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)-x)^{2}}{2(\xi^{\prime}(q_{n})-\xi^{\prime}(q_{n-1}))}\\
-u_{n}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)-m\,\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)\bigr)\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}m=\sqrt{2\pi(\xi^{\prime}(q_{n})-\xi^{\prime}(q_{n-1}))}\,e^{u_{n}\Phi_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},x)}.

The two terms cancel exactly, and we conclude that

\log\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q_{n},\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)})\mid\mathrm{Skel}_{n-1}=(0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),\ldots,0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta))\Bigr]\leq Nu_{n}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)-f)+O_{\varepsilon}(N).

Step 2: reduction to a good event.

Set
\delta_{\varepsilon}:=\varepsilon^{1/2}
and let
\mathrm{Good}(\varepsilon)\subset[-1+\delta_{\varepsilon},1-\delta_{\varepsilon}]^{N}

be the set of
\mathbf{m}
such that

(\mathbf{m}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)},\mathbf{m})\in\mathrm{SUSY}_{n}(\zeta,\varepsilon),

and

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m_{i})-m_{i}\,\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m_{i})\bigr)\\
+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{q_{n}}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t\in[-f-\varepsilon,-f+\varepsilon].

In particular, every
\mathbf{m}\in\mathrm{Good}(\varepsilon)
satisfies

\mathrm{dist}(\zeta_{\mathbf{m}},\zeta_{\star})\leq\varepsilon.

Restricting the Kac–Rice integral to
\mathrm{Good}(\varepsilon)
and using the equality case
of Lemma
3.4
(the last energy constraint holds with
f^{\prime}

in place of
f
because
|f^{\prime}-f|\leq\varepsilon
), we get

\displaystyle\log\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q_{n},\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)})\mid\mathrm{Skel}_{n-1}=(0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),\ldots,0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta))\Bigr]

\displaystyle\quad\geq u_{n}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},m_{i}^{(n-1)})-m_{i}^{(n-1)}\,\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},m_{i}^{(n-1)})\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{N}{2}\log\bigl(2\pi(\xi^{\prime}(q_{n})-\xi^{\prime}(q_{n-1}))\bigr)+Nu_{n}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)-f)+\log I_{N,\varepsilon}+O_{\varepsilon}(N),

where

I_{N,\varepsilon}:=\int_{[-1,1]^{N}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m_{i})\,\exp\!\biggl(-\frac{(\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m_{i})-\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},m_{i}^{(n-1)}))^{2}}{2(\xi^{\prime}(q_{n})-\xi^{\prime}(q_{n-1}))}\\
-u_{n}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m_{i})-m_{i}\,\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m_{i})\bigr)\biggr)\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{Good}(\varepsilon)}(\mathbf{m})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m}.

Step 3: control of the good event under a truncated conditional product law.

For
a\in[-1+\delta_{\varepsilon},1-\delta_{\varepsilon}]
, define the truncated normalizing constant

Z_{\varepsilon}(a):=\int_{-1+\delta_{\varepsilon}}^{1-\delta_{\varepsilon}}\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)\,\exp\!\biggl(-\frac{(\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)-\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},a))^{2}}{2(\xi^{\prime}(q_{n})-\xi^{\prime}(q_{n-1}))}\\
-u_{n}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)-m\,\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)\bigr)\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}m,

and let
\pi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}m\mid a)
be the probability measure on

[-1+\delta_{\varepsilon},1-\delta_{\varepsilon}]
proportional to the integrand above.
Let

\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}^{(N)}:=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\pi_{\varepsilon}(\cdot\mid m_{i}^{(n-1)}).

Then

I_{N,\varepsilon}=\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{N}Z_{\varepsilon}(m_{i}^{(n-1)})\Bigr)\,\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}^{(N)}\bigl(\mathrm{Good}(\varepsilon)\bigr).

We now show that

(3.33)

\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}^{(N)}\bigl(\mathrm{Good}(\varepsilon)^{c}\bigr)\longrightarrow 0\qquad\text{as }N\to\infty.

To this end, let
\pi(\cdot\mid a)
denote the same kernel without truncation
(i.e., integrating over
[-1,1]
instead of
[-1+\delta_{\varepsilon},1-\delta_{\varepsilon}]
).
If

U:=\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},X_{q_{n-1}}),

then by (
2.45
) and Lemma
B.3
,

\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},\cdot)\#\pi(\cdot\mid a)=\mathrm{Law}(X_{q_{n}}\mid U=a).

Consequently,

\displaystyle\int m^{2}\,\pi(\mathrm{d}m\mid a)

\displaystyle=\chi_{1}(a),

\displaystyle\int m\,\pi(\mathrm{d}m\mid a)

\displaystyle=\chi_{3}(a)=a,

\displaystyle\int m\,\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)\,\pi(\mathrm{d}m\mid a)

\displaystyle=\chi_{2}(a),

\displaystyle\int\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)\,\pi(\mathrm{d}m\mid a)

\displaystyle=\chi_{4}(a),

\displaystyle\int\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)-m\,\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)\bigr)\,\pi(\mathrm{d}m\mid a)

\displaystyle=-\chi_{5}(a).

For each fixed
\varepsilon
, the corresponding truncated expectations

a\mapsto\int\psi(m)\,\pi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}m\mid a)

are continuous on
[-1+\delta_{\varepsilon},1-\delta_{\varepsilon}]
for every bounded continuous

\psi
, and differ from the untruncated ones by
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
uniformly on this
compact set. Using the weak law-matching input
(
3.7
)–(
3.10
), we therefore obtain the following target
means, all up to an
O_{\varepsilon}(N)
error:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}^{(N)}}\!\Bigl[\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}^{2}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=Nq_{n}+O_{\varepsilon}(N),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}^{(N)}}\!\Bigl[\sum_{i=1}^{N}(m_{i}-m_{i}^{(n-1)})m_{i}^{(k)}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=O_{\varepsilon}(N),\qquad 1\leq k\leq n-1,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}^{(N)}}\!\Bigl[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m_{i})\,m_{i}^{(k)}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=N\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{q_{n}}\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q_{k},X_{q_{k}})\bigr]+O_{\varepsilon}(N),

\displaystyle\hskip 120.00018pt1\leq k\leq n,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}^{(N)}}\!\Bigl[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{k},m_{i}^{(k)})\,m_{i}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=N\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{q_{k}}\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q_{n},X_{q_{n}})\bigr]+O_{\varepsilon}(N),

\displaystyle\hskip 120.00018pt1\leq k\leq n-1,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}^{(N)}}\!\Bigl[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m_{i})-m_{i}\,\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m_{i})\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=-N\,\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{\zeta}(q_{n},X_{q_{n}})]+O_{\varepsilon}(N).

In the last line, (
3.30
) turns the right-hand side into

-Nf-\frac{N}{2}\int_{0}^{1}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t-\frac{N}{2}\int_{0}^{q_{n}}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\,\mathrm{d}t+O_{\varepsilon}(N).

Since the coordinates are independent and uniformly bounded under

\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}^{(N)}
, Chebyshev’s inequality gives concentration around these
means. Because there are only finitely many scalar and bilinear constraints, it
follows that all of them hold with
\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}^{(N)}
-probability tending to

1
.

It remains to control the metric constraint on
\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}
.
Recall that
\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}
is the unique minimizer of
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})},\cdot)

on
\mathcal{P}([q_{\mathbf{m}},1])
, and that
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\zeta^{\prime})
depends on
\mu
only through

q_{\mu}=\int m^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu
and
\int h_{\zeta^{\prime}}(q_{\mu},m)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(m)
.
Set

\mu_{\varepsilon}:=\int_{-1+\delta_{\varepsilon}}^{1-\delta_{\varepsilon}}\pi_{\varepsilon}(\cdot\mid a)\,\mathrm{Law}(U)(\mathrm{d}a).

We show that
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})},\cdot)
is uniformly close
to
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu_{\varepsilon},\cdot)
, using only finitely many linear statistics of
the ancestors.

By the Legendre duality (
2.45
) and Lemma
B.3
,
the pushforward of
\pi(\cdot\mid a)
by
\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},\cdot)
is

\mathrm{Law}(X_{q_{n}}\mid U=a)
; hence

\int h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)\,\pi(\mathrm{d}m\mid a)=\chi_{2}(a)-\chi_{5}(a),\qquad\int m^{2}\,\pi(\mathrm{d}m\mid a)=\chi_{1}(a).

The truncated kernels
\pi_{\varepsilon}(\cdot\mid a)
differ from
\pi(\cdot\mid a)

by
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
uniformly on
[-1+\delta_{\varepsilon},1-\delta_{\varepsilon}]
.
Under
\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}^{(N)}
, the coordinates
m_{i}
are independent and
bounded, so for every fixed continuous
g
,

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}g(m_{i})-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int g\,\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varepsilon}(\cdot\mid m_{i}^{(n-1)})\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0\qquad\text{in }\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}^{(N)}\text{-probability}

by Chebyshev’s inequality (
\mathrm{Var}=O(1/N)
).
Applying this with
g(m)=m^{2}
and
g(m)=h_{\zeta^{\prime}}(q_{n},m)
, it suffices
to compare the
deterministic
averages

\frac{1}{N}\sum\int g\,\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varepsilon}(\cdot\mid m_{i}^{(n-1)})
with
\int g\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varepsilon}
.

For the reference measure
\zeta
and
g=h_{\zeta}(q_{n},\cdot)
:

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)\,\pi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}m\mid m_{i}^{(n-1)})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(\chi_{2}(m_{i}^{(n-1)})-\chi_{5}(m_{i}^{(n-1)})\bigr)+O_{\varepsilon}(1).

By the law-matching condition (
3.9
) applied to
\chi_{2}
and
\chi_{5}
,
this equals
\mathbb{E}[\chi_{2}(U)-\chi_{5}(U)]+O_{\varepsilon}(1)=\int h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varepsilon}(m)+O_{\varepsilon}(1)
.
Similarly, (
3.9
) applied to
\chi_{1}
gives

\frac{1}{N}\sum\chi_{1}(m_{i}^{(n-1)})=q_{n}+O(\varepsilon)
.

For a nearby measure
\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}([q_{n},1])
, the stability of the
Parisi PDE gives

\sup_{m\in[-1+\delta_{\varepsilon},1-\delta_{\varepsilon}]}|h_{\zeta^{\prime}}(q_{n},m)-h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)|\leq C\,\mathrm{dist}(\zeta^{\prime},\zeta)
. Since this bound is uniform in the ancestor
coordinates, we obtain

\sup_{\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}([q_{n},1])}\bigl|\mathrm{TAP}(\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})},\zeta^{\prime})-\mathrm{TAP}(\mu_{\varepsilon},\zeta^{\prime})\bigr|=O_{\varepsilon}(1)\qquad\text{in }\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}^{(N)}\text{-probability.}

Since
\mathcal{P}([q_{n},1])
is compact and
\zeta^{\prime}\mapsto\mathrm{TAP}(\mu_{\varepsilon},\zeta^{\prime})
is
strictly convex with unique minimizer
\zeta_{\star,\varepsilon}
, uniform
convergence of the functionals implies convergence of the minimizers:

\mathrm{dist}(\zeta_{\mathbf{m}},\zeta_{\star,\varepsilon})\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0\qquad\text{in }\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}^{(N)}\text{-probability.}

Finally, as in Step 2 of the proof of Proposition
2.2
,
the unique minimizer of
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\cdot)
for

\mu:=\mathrm{Law}(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q_{n},X_{q_{n}}))
is
\zeta_{\star}
.
Since
\mu_{\varepsilon}\Rightarrow\mu
and
\int m^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varepsilon}\to q_{n}
as
\varepsilon\to 0

(by dominated convergence), Lemma
B.9
gives

\mathrm{dist}(\zeta_{\star,\varepsilon},\zeta_{\star})\to 0
.
After decreasing
\varepsilon
if necessary,

\mathrm{dist}(\zeta_{\star,\varepsilon},\zeta_{\star})\leq\varepsilon/2
, and hence

\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}^{(N)}\bigl(\mathrm{dist}(\zeta_{\mathbf{m}},\zeta_{\star})>\varepsilon\bigr)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0.

Combining this with the concentration estimates above proves
(
3.33
).

Step 4: conclusion.

For
a\in[-1+\delta_{\varepsilon},1-\delta_{\varepsilon}]
, let
Z(a)
denote the normalization
without truncation:

Z(a):=\int_{-1}^{1}\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)\,\exp\!\biggl(-\frac{(\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)-\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},a))^{2}}{2(\xi^{\prime}(q_{n})-\xi^{\prime}(q_{n-1}))}\\
-u_{n}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)-m\,\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n},m)\bigr)\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}m.

By the integral identity from Step 1,

\log Z(a)=\tfrac{1}{2}\log\bigl(2\pi(\xi^{\prime}(q_{n})-\xi^{\prime}(q_{n-1}))\bigr)+u_{n}\Phi_{\zeta}\bigl(q_{n-1},\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},a)\bigr)\\
=\tfrac{1}{2}\log\bigl(2\pi(\xi^{\prime}(q_{n})-\xi^{\prime}(q_{n-1}))\bigr)-u_{n}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},a)-a\,\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},a)\bigr).

Moreover, the truncation error satisfies

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(\log Z_{\varepsilon}(m_{i}^{(n-1)})-\log Z(m_{i}^{(n-1)})\bigr)=O_{\varepsilon}(1),

since
\log Z_{\varepsilon}(a)-\log Z(a)=O_{\varepsilon}(1)
uniformly on
[-1+\delta_{\varepsilon},1-\delta_{\varepsilon}]
. Therefore,

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log Z_{\varepsilon}(m_{i}^{(n-1)})=\frac{N}{2}\log\bigl(2\pi(\xi^{\prime}(q_{n})-\xi^{\prime}(q_{n-1}))\bigr)\\
-u_{n}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},m_{i}^{(n-1)})-m_{i}^{(n-1)}\,\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q_{n-1},m_{i}^{(n-1)})\bigr)+O_{\varepsilon}(N).

Since
\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}^{(N)}(\mathrm{Good}(\varepsilon))\to 1
, we have

\log I_{N,\varepsilon}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log Z_{\varepsilon}(m_{i}^{(n-1)})+o_{N}(N)

for every fixed
\varepsilon
. Inserting the last two displays into the lower bound from
Step 2 yields

\log\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q_{n},\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)})\mid\mathrm{Skel}_{n-1}=(0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),\ldots,0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta))\Bigr]\geq Nu_{n}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)-f)+O_{\varepsilon}(N).

Together with Step 1, this gives

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q_{n},\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)})\mid\mathrm{Skel}_{n-1}=(0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),\ldots,0,N\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta))\Bigr]=u_{n}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)-f).

Finally, assumption
(i)
says that

\mathcal{P}^{(n)}(\bm{u};\bm{q})=\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),

and by definition
u_{n}=\zeta([0,q_{n}))
, so the right-hand side is

u_{n}\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{(n)}(\bm{u};\bm{q})-f\bigr)=\mathcal{C}_{f}^{(n)}(\bm{u};\bm{q}).

This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
The supersymmetric ansatz

This appendix collects the heuristic supersymmetric computations that motivate the rigorous proofs of Sections
2
and
3
. The discussion in this section is purely formal and is not intended to be rigorous.

A.1.
Ansatz for the annealed complexity

Writing the complexity as a partition function

Let
f\in\mathbb{R}
,
q\in(0,1)
, and
u\in(0,1)
. Let
\zeta\in\mathrm{Prefix}_{2}(u,q)
satisfy the assumptions of Proposition
2.2
, and note that
u=\zeta(\{0\})
. Recall
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(f,\zeta,q)
from the statement of Proposition
2.2
. Set
\zeta_{\star}=\zeta|_{(q,1]}+\zeta([0,q])\delta_{q}
.

Replacing the condition
F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\in N(f-\varepsilon,f+\varepsilon)
by the sharper constraint
F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})=Nf
, the Kac–Rice formula yields (formally)

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{N}_{0}(f,\zeta,q)]\approx\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{[-1,1]^{N}}|\det(\nabla^{2}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))|\,\delta_{\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}}(0)\,\delta_{F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})}(Nf)\,\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,0)}(\mathbf{m})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m}\right].

We introduce a simplified quantity obtained by dropping the absolute value of the determinant: formally set

\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{0}(f,\zeta,q)=\int_{[-1,1]^{N}}\det(\nabla^{2}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}))\,\delta_{\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})}(0)\,\delta_{F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})}(Nf)\,\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,0)}(\mathbf{m})\delta_{\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}-\zeta_{\star}}(0)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m}.

(As we shall see, the condition
\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}=\zeta_{\star}
implies that the bulk of the spectrum of the Hessian has a fixed sign (positive in our convention), which justifies this simplification.) For a matrix
A\in\mathcal{M}_{N}(\mathbb{R})
, we use the standard Grassmann representation

(A.1)

\det A=\int e^{-\bar{\psi}^{\top}A\psi}\mathrm{d}\bar{\psi}\mathrm{d}\psi,

where
\mathrm{d}\bar{\psi}\mathrm{d}\psi=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathrm{d}\bar{\psi}_{i}\mathrm{d}\psi_{i}
denotes the Berezin measure. The Dirac delta function admits the integral representation

(A.2)

\delta_{0}(y)=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\int_{i\mathbb{R}}e^{xy}\mathrm{d}x.

Applying (
A.1
) to
\nabla^{2}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})
and (
A.2
) to
\partial_{i}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})
for each
i\in[N]
, as well as to
F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})-Nf
, and performing the change of variables
x\mapsto-x
(which preserves the contour
(i\mathbb{R})^{N}
), we obtain

(A.3)

\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{0}(f,\zeta,q)]=\frac{1}{(2i\pi)^{N+1}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int e^{S(\mathbf{m},x,v,\bar{\psi},\psi)}\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,0)}(\mathbf{m})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}\bar{\psi}\mathrm{d}\psi\right],

where
S
is the action

S(\mathbf{m},x,v,\bar{\psi},\psi)=\,x\cdot\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})-\bar{\psi}^{\top}\nabla^{2}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})\psi+v(F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m})-Nf),

with
x\in(i\mathbb{R})^{N}
and
v\in i\mathbb{R}
.

The BRST supersymmetry

Consider the infinitesimal fermionic transformation with odd parameter
\varepsilon
:

\delta m_{i}=\varepsilon\psi_{i},\quad\delta x_{i}=-\varepsilon v\psi_{i},\quad\delta\bar{\psi}_{i}=\varepsilon x_{i},\quad\delta\psi_{i}=0.

A standard computation shows that the Berezinian (super-Jacobian) of this change of variables equals
1
and that

\delta S=0.

Consequently, both the integrand and the measure are invariant under this transformation. Hence, for any sufficiently regular observable
O=O(\mathbf{m},x,\bar{\psi},\psi)
,

0=\int\delta(Oe^{S})=\int(\delta O)e^{S},

which yields the Ward identities

\int(\delta O)\,e^{S}=0.

Taking

O=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bar{\psi}_{i}m_{i}
, we obtain

(A.4)

\int\langle\bar{\psi},\psi\rangle\,e^{S}=\int\langle x,\mathbf{m}\rangle\,e^{S}.

A supersymmetric saddle is a critical point of the (disorder-averaged) effective action that satisfies all Ward identities simultaneously.

Computing the annealed complexity

Differentiating the TAP correction (using
q=\frac{1}{N}\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}
and
\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}=\zeta|_{(q,1]}+\zeta([0,q])\delta_{q}
, see Lemma
B.8
) gives

(A.5)

\partial_{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Phi_{\zeta}^{*}(q,\cdot)(m_{j})+\frac{1}{2}N\int_{q}^{1}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t\right)=k_{\zeta}(q,m_{i}),

where
k_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})
is as in (
1.22
). Furthermore, for every
i,j\in[N]
,

(A.6)

\partial_{ij}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Phi_{\zeta}^{*}(q,\cdot)(m_{k})+\frac{1}{2}N\int_{q}^{1}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t\right)=\left(\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})+\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t\right)\mathbf{1}_{i=j}\\
+O\left(\frac{1}{N}\right).

Taking expectations in (
A.3
) and applying (
A.5
)–(
A.6
) yields

(A.7)

\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{0}(f,\zeta,q)]=\frac{1}{(2i\pi)^{N+1}}\int e^{\mathbb{E}[S(\mathbf{m},x,v,\bar{\psi},\psi)]+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Var}[S(\mathbf{m},x,v,\bar{\psi},\psi)]}\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,0)}(\mathbf{m})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}\bar{\psi}\mathrm{d}\psi.

Explicitly,

\mathbb{E}[S(\mathbf{m},x,v,\bar{\psi},\psi)]=-\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}k_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})-\sum_{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}\psi_{i}\Bigl(\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})+\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t\Bigr)\\
+v\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})-N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q)-Nf\right)+O(1),

and

\mathrm{Var}[S(\mathbf{m},x,v,\bar{\psi},\psi)]=v^{2}N\xi(q)+\xi^{\prime}(q)\,\|x\|^{2}+\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)}{N}\,\langle\mathbf{m},x\rangle^{2}-\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)}{N}\,\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bar{\psi}_{i}\psi_{i}\right)^{2}+2v\xi^{\prime}(q)\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}m_{i}\\
+\frac{2\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)}{N}\!\left[\langle\bar{\psi},x\rangle\langle\mathbf{m},\psi\rangle+\langle\bar{\psi},\mathbf{m}\rangle\langle x,\psi\rangle+v\langle\bar{\psi},\mathbf{m}\rangle\langle\mathbf{m},\psi\rangle\right]+O\Bigl(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\Bigr).

Connection to random matrix theory

Let
t>0
. Let
G
be an
N\times N
GOE matrix with parameter
t
, i.e.
G
is real symmetric and

(G_{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\ \text{i.i.d. }\mathcal{N}(0,t/N),\qquad(G_{ii})_{1\leq i\leq N}\ \text{i.i.d. }\mathcal{N}(0,2t/N),

independent across
i\leq j
.
Equivalently,
G
has density proportional to
\exp\{-\frac{N}{4t}\mathrm{Tr}(G^{2})\}
. Let
D_{N}
be a diagonal matrix. By (
A.1
),

\det(G+D_{N})=\int e^{-\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}(G_{ij}+D_{ii}\delta_{i,j})\bar{\psi}_{i}\psi_{j}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathrm{d}\bar{\psi}_{i}\mathrm{d}\psi_{i}.

Taking expectations yields

(A.8)

\mathbb{E}[\det(G+D_{N})]=\int e^{-\sum_{i=1}^{N}D_{ii}\bar{\psi}_{i}\psi_{i}-\frac{t}{2N}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bar{\psi}_{i}\psi_{i})^{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathrm{d}\bar{\psi}_{i}\mathrm{d}\psi_{i}.

The quadratic term in the exponent can be linearized via the Hubbard–Stratonovich transform:

(A.9)

e^{-\frac{t}{2N}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bar{\psi}_{i}\psi_{i})^{2}}=\sqrt{\frac{N}{2\pi t}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\frac{N}{2t}z^{2}+iz\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bar{\psi}_{i}\psi_{i}}\mathrm{d}z.

Substituting (
A.9
) into (
A.8
) and integrating out the fermionic variables gives

\mathbb{E}[\det(G+D_{N})]=\sqrt{\frac{N}{2\pi t}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\frac{N}{2t}z^{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}(d_{i}-iz)\mathrm{d}z.

Suppose now that
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{D_{ii}}
converges to a probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
with support bounded away from
0
. The integral representation shows that
\mathbb{E}[\det(G+D_{N})]>0
, and by the steepest descent method one can establish that

\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}[\det(G+D_{N})]=\frac{\omega_{\mu,t}^{2}(0)}{2t}+\int\log|x-\omega_{\mu,t}(0)|\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+o_{N}(1),

where
\omega_{\mu,t}(0)\in\mathbb{C}^{+}\cup\mathbb{R}
is the subordination function of
\mu
at time
t
and location
0
; its definition is recalled in Lemma
C.2
in the Appendix. In particular, taking the saddle point in (
A.9
), we deduce that whenever
\ell(\mu\boxplus\sigma_{t})>0
, the typical value of
\langle\bar{\psi},\psi\rangle
in (
A.8
) satisfies

(A.10)

\frac{1}{N}\langle\bar{\psi},\psi\rangle\approx\frac{1}{t}\omega_{\mu,t}(0).

The value of
\omega_{\mu,t}(0)
admits a variational characterization. Recall that the left edge of the free convolution is given by

\ell(\mu\boxplus\sigma_{t})=\inf\left\{x+tG_{\mu}(x):\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu(\lambda)}{(x-\lambda)^{2}}\geq\frac{1}{t}\right\}.

If
\ell(\mu\boxplus\sigma_{t})>0
, then
\omega_{\mu,t}(0)\in\mathbb{R}
and

\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}[\det(G+D_{N})]=\inf_{\lambda\leq\omega_{\mu,t}(\ell(\mu\boxplus\sigma_{t}))}\left\{\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2t}+\int\log(x-\lambda)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right\}+o_{N}(1).

Since
\zeta|_{(q,1]}+\zeta([0,q])\delta_{q}
is the minimizer of
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})},\cdot)
, Lemma
B.6
gives

\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\mathbb{E}[\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}(q,\cdot)^{-2}]\leq 1.

Since
\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}=Nq
, we will show in Lemma
2.7
that this implies

\omega_{T_{\zeta}\#\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})},\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)}(0)=\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t,

where
T_{\zeta}={\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}}+\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t
. In view of (
A.10
), we therefore expect

(A.11)

\frac{1}{N}\langle\bar{\psi},\psi\rangle\approx\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t.

Identifying the supersymmetric saddle via the Ward identities

Combining the Ward identity (
A.4
) with (
A.11
), we seek a saddle satisfying

(A.12)

\langle\bar{\psi},\psi\rangle=\langle x,\mathbf{m}\rangle=N\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t.

The remaining Ward identities further enforce
\langle\bar{\psi},x\rangle=0
,
\langle\mathbf{m},\psi\rangle=0
,
\langle\bar{\psi},\mathbf{m}\rangle=0
, and
\langle x,\psi\rangle=0
. We also impose the constraint
\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}=Nq
. Let
\mathcal{F}
denote the subspace of
(\mathbf{m},x,v,\bar{\psi},\psi)
satisfying all these conditions, and define the restricted partition function

I:=\int e^{\mathbb{E}[S(\mathbf{m},x,v,\bar{\psi},\psi)]+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Var}[S(\mathbf{m},x,v,\bar{\psi},\psi)]}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathbf{m},x,v,\bar{\psi},\psi)\,\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,0)}(\mathbf{m})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}\bar{\psi}\mathrm{d}\psi.

On
\mathcal{F}
, the exponent simplifies to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{m},x,v,\bar{\psi},\psi)
, where

\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{m},x,v,\bar{\psi},\psi)=-\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}k_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})-\sum_{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}\psi_{i}\left(\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})+\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t\right)\\
-v\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})+N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q)+Nf\right)+\frac{1}{2}v^{2}N\xi(q)+\frac{1}{2}\xi^{\prime}(q)\,\|x\|^{2}+v\xi^{\prime}(q)\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}m_{i}.

The constraints are enforced via Lagrange multipliers. One can verify that

I\approx\int e^{\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{m},x,v,\bar{\psi},\psi)}e^{\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)(\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bar{\psi}_{i}\psi_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}m_{i}\right)}\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{SUSY}_{1}(\zeta,0)}(\mathbf{m})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}\bar{\psi}\mathrm{d}\psi:=I^{\prime}.

Integrating out the fermionic variables
\bar{\psi}
and
\psi
produces the determinant of the diagonal matrix with entries
\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})
,
i\in[N]
, so that

I^{\prime}=\int\prod_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})\,\exp\biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(-x_{i}\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})+v\xi^{\prime}(q)x_{i}m_{i}\bigr)\\
+\tfrac{1}{2}\xi^{\prime}(q)\|x\|^{2}-v\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})+N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q)+Nf\Bigr)+\tfrac{1}{2}Nv^{2}\xi(q)\biggr\}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v,

where we have used the identity
k_{\zeta}(q,m)=\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,m)+m\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t
. Integrating over
x
then gives

I^{\prime}=\int e^{\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbf{m},v)}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m}\mathrm{d}v,

where

\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbf{m},v)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})-v\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})+N\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q)+Nf\right)+\frac{1}{2}v^{2}N\xi(q)\\
-\frac{1}{2\xi^{\prime}(q)}\|\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,\mathbf{m})-v\xi^{\prime}(q)\mathbf{m}\|^{2}.

A direct computation identifies the saddle as
v=u
(i.e.,
v=\zeta(\{0\})
), and yields

(A.13)

\log I^{\prime}=uN\left(\Phi_{\zeta}(0,0)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t-f\right)+o(N).

Consistency check

Denote by
\mu
the candidate measure identified above:

\mathrm{d}\mu(m)\propto\partial_{mm}h_{\zeta}(q,m)\,e^{-\frac{1}{2\xi^{\prime}(q)}\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,m)^{2}-u(h_{\zeta}(q,m)-\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,m)m)}\mathbf{1}_{[-1,1]}(m)\,\mathrm{d}m.

Let
\nu
be the pushforward of
\mu
under the map
\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,\cdot)
. A key observation is that
\nu=\mathrm{Law}(X_{q})
, where
X_{t}
is the Auffinger–Chen process

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\mathrm{d}B_{t},\\
X_{0}=0.\end{cases}

Using properties of the Auffinger–Chen process (see Section
B
) together with the optimality conditions on
\zeta
(see the statement of Proposition
2.2
), one can verify that
\mu
is indeed a consistent solution. We omit the details here, as a complete treatment is given in Section
2
.

Throughout the remainder of the paper, the Ward identity (
A.12
) serves as a central guide for the computations.

A.2.
Ansatz for the conditional annealed complexity

The supersymmetric computation of Section
A.1
can be extended to compute the number of critical points of a hierarchical system of replicas. For brevity, we record only the resulting ansatz.

Partition function

Let
f\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
n\geq 2
and let
(u_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
and
(q_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
be strictly increasing sequences of numbers in
(0,1)
. Let
\zeta\in\mathrm{Prefix}_{n+1}((u_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n},(q_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n})
where
\mathrm{Prefix}_{n+1}
is as in Definition
1.2
. Suppose that
\zeta
satisfies the assumptions of Proposition
3.3
.

Set
f_{1}=\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta),\ldots,f_{n-1}=\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)
and
f_{n}=f
. We count the number of critical points
\mathbf{m}^{(n)}
of free energy level
f
of square radius
q_{n}
that are in the band orthogonal to some
\mathbf{m}^{(n-1)}
critical point of level
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)
of square radius
q_{n-1}
, which is the band orthogonal to some
\mathbf{m}^{(n-2)}
critical point of level
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta)
of square radius
q_{n-2}
, etc. Dropping the absolute value of the determinant, a good guess for the number of critical points of this hierarchical subsystem is

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{([-1,1]^{N})^{n}}\prod_{k=1}^{n}\det(\nabla^{2}F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}^{(k)}))\right.\\
\times\left.\prod_{k=1}^{n}\left(\delta_{\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}^{(k)})}(0)\delta_{F_{\mathrm{TAP},\zeta}(\mathbf{m}^{(k)})}(Nf_{k})\mathbf{1}_{(\mathbf{m}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{m}^{(n)})\in\mathrm{SUSY}_{n}(\zeta,0)}\right)\prod_{k=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{m}^{(k)}\right].

As in Section
A.1
, one can introduce Lagrange fields
x^{(k)}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and Lagrange parameters
\Delta_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
, for
k\in[n]
and fermionic fields
\psi^{(k)}
,
\bar{\psi}^{(k)}
.

Summarizing the ansatz

The Ward identities suggest that for the optimum,

(A.14)

\langle x^{(i)},\mathbf{m}^{(j)}\rangle=0\quad\text{and}\quad\langle\bar{\psi}^{(i)},{\psi}^{(j)}\rangle=0\quad\text{for every $i\neq j\in[n]$,}

and

\langle x^{(i)},\mathbf{m}^{(i)}\rangle=\langle\bar{\psi}^{(i)},\psi^{(i)}\rangle\quad\text{for every $i\in[n]$}.

Moreover, as in Section
A.1
, the fact that
\zeta_{\mathbf{m}^{(k)}}=\zeta|_{(q_{k},1]}+\zeta([0,q_{k}])\delta_{q_{k}}
gives

(A.15)

\langle\bar{\psi}^{(i)},\psi^{(i)}\rangle=N\int_{q_{i}}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t=\langle x^{(i)},\mathbf{m}^{(i)}\rangle.

Moreover, the Lagrange multipliers are expected to be given by

\Delta_{n}=\zeta([0,q_{n}))\quad\text{and for every $i\in[n-1]$,}\quad\Delta_{i}=-\zeta(\{q_{i}\}).

Observe that for every
i\in[n]
, one has
\Delta_{i}+\cdots+\Delta_{n}=\zeta([0,q_{i}))
.

Appendix B
Properties of the Parisi PDE

B.1.
The Auffinger–Chen SDE

Recall from Assumption
1.1
that
\xi
is smooth, strictly convex,
\xi(0)=0
and
\xi^{\prime}(0)=0
.

The Parisi PDE (
B.4
) is a semilinear parabolic equation
whose quadratic gradient nonlinearity places it in the class of
Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equations arising in stochastic optimal control.
We now make this connection precise.

A
feedback control
is a Borel-measurable map

\alpha\colon[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that,
for every initial condition
(t,x)\in[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}
,
the stochastic differential equation

(B.1)

\mathrm{d}X_{s}=\zeta\bigl([0,s]\bigr)\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)\,\alpha(s,X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)}\,\mathrm{d}B_{s},\qquad X_{t}=x,

admits a unique strong solution
(X_{s})_{s\in[t,1]}
satisfying
the square-integrability condition

(B.2)

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\int_{t}^{1}\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)\,\zeta\bigl([0,s]\bigr)\,\alpha(s,X_{s})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]<\infty.

Denote the class of all such feedback controls by
\mathcal{A}
.
Consider the stochastic optimal control problem of maximizing the
payoff functional

(B.3)

\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x)=\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\;\mathbb{E}\!\left[\log\bigl(2\cosh(X_{1})\bigr)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{t}^{1}\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)\,\zeta\bigl([0,s]\bigr)\,\alpha(s,X_{s})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\;\middle|\;X_{t}=x\right],

where
(X_{s})_{s\in[t,1]}
solves (
B.1
)
under the feedback control
\alpha
.
By the dynamic programming principle, the value
function
\Phi_{\zeta}
satisfies the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation

\partial_{t}\Phi_{\zeta}+\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}{2}\,\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}+\sup_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl\{\zeta\bigl([0,t]\bigr)\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,a\;\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\zeta\bigl([0,t]\bigr)\,a^{2}\Bigr\}=0.

Optimizing the Hamiltonian pointwise in
a
yields a maximizer

a^{\star}=\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x)
(which is unique for time
t
such that
\zeta([0,t])>0
), and substitution recovers the
Parisi PDE

(B.4)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x)=-\dfrac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}{2}\Bigl(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x)+\zeta\bigl([0,t]\bigr)\bigl(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x)\bigr)^{2}\Bigr),\\[6.0pt]
\Phi_{\zeta}(1,x)=\log\bigl(2\cosh x\bigr).\end{cases}

A standard verification argument then confirms that
\Phi_{\zeta}

coincides with the value function (
B.3
) and that the
optimal feedback control is

(B.5)

\alpha^{\star}(t,x)=\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x).

Substituting (
B.5
) into (
B.1
)
gives the closed-loop dynamics

(B.6)

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\zeta\bigl([0,t]\bigr)\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t},\qquad X_{0}=0,

which is called the
Auffinger–Chen SDE
.
In particular, the following differential identities hold.

Lemma B.1

(Differential identities)
.

Let
X_{t}
denote the Auffinger–Chen process (
B.6
). Then:

(i)

Define

Y_{t}:=\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})-\Phi_{\zeta}(0,X_{0})-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)\,\zeta([0,s])(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(s,X_{s}))^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s-\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(s,X_{s})\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)}\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}.

Then
\mathrm{d}Y_{t}=0
, i.e.
Y_{t}
is a.s. constant in
t
.

(ii)

The process
\bigl(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\bigr)_{t\in[0,1]}
is a martingale.

(iii)

The second derivative along the optimal trajectory satisfies

\mathrm{d}\bigl(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\bigr)=-\zeta([0,t])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\bigl(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\bigr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\,\partial_{xxx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}.

(iv)

Moreover,

\mathrm{d}\bigl(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\bigr)^{2}=2\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\,\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\bigl(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\bigr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Proof.

We first establish
(i)
. By Itô’s formula, for a smooth
f(t,x)
,

\mathrm{d}f(t,X_{t})=\partial_{t}f(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\partial_{x}f(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}X_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\,\partial_{xx}f(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}[X]_{t}.

Applying this to
f=\Phi_{\zeta}
and using
\mathrm{d}[X]_{t}=\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t
together with the optimal dynamics

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\zeta([0,t])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t},

we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}X_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\,\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\partial_{t}\Phi_{\zeta}+\zeta([0,t])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\bigl(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}\bigr)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}\Bigr)(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\,\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}.

Invoking the Parisi PDE (
B.4
) to substitute for
\partial_{t}\Phi_{\zeta}
, the drift simplifies to

\frac{1}{2}\,\zeta([0,t])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\bigl(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\bigr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t,

and therefore

\mathrm{d}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})=\frac{1}{2}\,\zeta([0,t])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t}))^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}.

By construction of
Y_{t}
, this is exactly equivalent to
\mathrm{d}Y_{t}=0
.

We next prove
(ii)
. Applying Itô’s formula to
f=\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}
yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\bigl(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\partial_{tx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}X_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\,\partial_{xxx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\partial_{tx}\Phi_{\zeta}+\zeta([0,t])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}\,\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\partial_{xxx}\Phi_{\zeta}\Bigr)(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\,\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}.

Differentiating (
B.4
) in
x
gives

\partial_{tx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x)=-\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}{2}\Bigl(\partial_{xxx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x)+2\,\zeta([0,t])\,\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x)\,\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x)\Bigr).

Substituting this identity into the drift term above cancels it identically, leaving

\mathrm{d}\bigl(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\bigr)=\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\,\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}.

Hence
\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})
is a local martingale; under the standing integrability conditions ensuring square-integrability of the stochastic integral, it is a martingale.

We now prove
(iii)
. Set

V(t,x):=\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x).

Applying Itô’s formula to
V(t,X_{t})
gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}V(t,X_{t})

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}V(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\partial_{x}V(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}X_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\,\partial_{xx}V(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}[X]_{t}

\displaystyle=\partial_{txx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\partial_{xxx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}X_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\,\partial_{xxxx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Using the optimal dynamics

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\zeta([0,t])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t},

we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}V(t,X_{t})

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\partial_{txx}\Phi_{\zeta}+\zeta([0,t])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}\,\partial_{xxx}\Phi_{\zeta}+\frac{1}{2}\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\partial_{xxxx}\Phi_{\zeta}\Bigr)(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\,\partial_{xxx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}.

Next differentiate (
B.4
) twice in
x
. Since

\partial_{xx}\bigl(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}\bigr)^{2}=2\bigl(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}\bigr)^{2}+2\,\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}\,\partial_{xxx}\Phi_{\zeta},

we have

\partial_{txx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x)=-\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}{2}\Bigl(\partial_{xxxx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x)+2\,\zeta([0,t])\Bigl((\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x))^{2}+\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x)\,\partial_{xxx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x)\Bigr)\Bigr).

Substituting this into the drift above, the
\partial_{xxxx}\Phi_{\zeta}
terms cancel,
and the
\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}\,\partial_{xxx}\Phi_{\zeta}
terms cancel as well, leaving

\mathrm{d}V(t,X_{t})=-\zeta([0,t])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\bigl(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\bigr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\,\partial_{xxx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}B_{t},

which is exactly the claimed identity in
(iii)
.

Finally,
(iv)
is an immediate consequence of Itô’s formula applied to the square of the martingale in
(ii)
, namely

\mathrm{d}(u_{t}^{2})=2u_{t}\,\mathrm{d}u_{t}+\mathrm{d}[u]_{t}

with
u_{t}=\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})
.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Let
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([0,1])
and fix
q\in[0,1]
.
Let
(X_{t})_{t\in[q,1]}
solve the Auffinger–Chen SDE

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\zeta([0,t])\,\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}.

Write
u(t,y):=\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,y)
. Then for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

(B.7)

\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,x)=1-\zeta\bigl([0,q)\bigr)\,u(q,x)^{2}-\int_{[q,1]}\mathbb{E}\!\left[u(t,X_{t})^{2}\,\middle|\,X_{q}=x\right]\zeta(\mathrm{d}t).

Consequently,

\mathbb{E}\big[\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,X_{q})\big]=1-\zeta\bigl([0,q)\bigr)\,\mathbb{E}\big[u(q,X_{q})^{2}\big]-\int_{[q,1]}\mathbb{E}\big[u(t,X_{t})^{2}\big]\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t).

Moreover, if
\mathbb{E}[u(t,X_{t})^{2}]=t
for
\zeta
-a.e.
t\in[q,1]

and also
\mathbb{E}[u(q,X_{q})^{2}]=q
,
then

\mathbb{E}\big[\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,X_{q})\big]=1-\zeta\bigl([0,q)\bigr)\,q-\int_{[q,1]}t\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t)=\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t.

Proof.

Let
u(t,y)=\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,y)
and set

V(t):=\mathbb{E}\!\left[u(t,X_{t})^{2}\mid X_{q}=x\right]
.
From Lemma
B.1
(iii)
,

\mathrm{d}\big(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\big)=-\zeta([0,t])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\big(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\big)^{2}\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\,\partial_{xxx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\mathrm{d}B_{t}.

Taking conditional expectation given
X_{q}=x
and integrating from
q
to
1
yields

(B.8)

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(1,X_{1})\mid X_{q}=x\right]-\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,x)=-\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\big(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\big)^{2}\mid X_{q}=x\right]\mathrm{d}t.

From Lemma
B.1
(iv)
,

\mathrm{d}\big(u(t,X_{t})^{2}\big)=2\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\,u(t,X_{t})\,\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\big(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\big)^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

so
V
is absolutely continuous on
[q,1]
with

(B.9)

V^{\prime}(t)=\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\big(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\big)^{2}\mid X_{q}=x\right].

Substituting (
B.9
) into (
B.8
) gives

(B.10)

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(1,X_{1})\mid X_{q}=x\right]-\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,x)=-\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\,V^{\prime}(t)\mathrm{d}t=-\int_{(q,1]}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}V(t).

Since
V
is continuous, Stieltjes integration by parts yields

\int_{(q,1]}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}V(t)=\zeta([0,1])\,V(1)-\zeta([0,q])\,V(q)-\int_{(q,1]}V(t)\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t).

Using
\zeta([0,1])=1
and decomposing

\zeta([0,q])=\zeta([0,q))+\zeta(\{q\})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(1,X_{1})\mid X_{q}=x\right]-\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,x)

\displaystyle=-V(1)+\zeta([0,q))\,V(q)+\zeta(\{q\})\,V(q)+\int_{(q,1]}V(t)\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t)

(B.11)

\displaystyle=-V(1)+\zeta([0,q))\,V(q)+\int_{[q,1]}V(t)\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t),

where the last step recombines the atom
\zeta(\{q\})\,V(q)
with the integral
over
(q,1]
into a single integral over
[q,1]
.

Now
\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(1,y)=1-\tanh^{2}(y)=1-u(1,y)^{2}
, so

\mathbb{E}[\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(1,X_{1})\mid X_{q}=x]=1-V(1)
.
Also
V(q)=u(q,x)^{2}
.
Substituting into (
B.11
) and rearranging:

\displaystyle\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,x)

\displaystyle=\bigl(1-V(1)\bigr)+V(1)-\zeta([0,q))\,u(q,x)^{2}-\int_{[q,1]}V(t)\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t)

\displaystyle=1-\zeta\bigl([0,q)\bigr)\,u(q,x)^{2}-\int_{[q,1]}V(t)\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t),

which is (
B.7
).

The second statement follows by taking expectation over
X_{q}
.
For the final claim, substitute
\mathbb{E}[u(t,\allowbreak X_{t})^{2}]=t
and
\mathbb{E}[u(q,\allowbreak X_{q})^{2}]=q
to get

\mathbb{E}\big[\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,X_{q})\big]=1-\zeta\bigl([0,q)\bigr)\,q-\int_{[q,1]}t\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t).

To verify this equals
\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t
, write

1-\zeta\bigl([0,q)\bigr)\,q-\int_{[q,1]}t\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t)=1-\int_{[0,q)}q\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t)-\int_{[q,1]}t\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t).

More directly, note that
\int_{[0,1]}1\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t)=1
and therefore

1-\zeta([0,q))\,q-\int_{[q,1]}t\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t)=\int_{[0,q)}(1-q)\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t)+\int_{[q,1]}(1-t)\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t).

Using the layer-cake identity

\int_{A}(1-t)\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t)=\int_{A}\int_{t}^{1}\mathrm{d}s\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t)

and Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\int_{[0,q)}(1-q)\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t)+\int_{[q,1]}(1-t)\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t)

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,q)}\int_{q}^{1}\mathrm{d}s\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t)+\int_{[q,1]}\int_{t}^{1}\mathrm{d}s\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t)

\displaystyle=\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,q))\mathrm{d}s+\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([q,s])\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{q}^{1}\bigl(\zeta([0,q))+\zeta([q,s])\bigr)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,s])\mathrm{d}s,

as claimed.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Let
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([0,1])
and let
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
solve the Auffinger–Chen SDE

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\,\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t},\qquad X_{0}=0.

Fix
0\leq s<t\leq 1
and assume that

\zeta((s,t))=0,

so that
\zeta([0,u])=\zeta([0,s])
for every
u\in[s,t)
. Set
\alpha:=\zeta([0,s])
.
Then, conditionally on
X_{s}=x
, the law of
X_{t}
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure and satisfies

\mathrm{Law}(X_{t}\mid X_{s}=x)(\mathrm{d}y)\ \propto\ \exp\!\left(-\frac{(y-x)^{2}}{2(\xi^{\prime}(t)-\xi^{\prime}(s))}+\alpha\,\Phi_{\zeta}(t,y)\right)\mathrm{d}y.

Equivalently, writing
p^{BM}_{s,t}(x,y)
for the transition density of the inhomogeneous Brownian motion

\mathrm{d}Y_{u}=\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(u)}\,\mathrm{d}W_{u}
(so that
\mathrm{Var}(Y_{t}-Y_{s})=\xi^{\prime}(t)-\xi^{\prime}(s)
), one has

p_{s,t}(x,y)=\frac{e^{\alpha\Phi_{\zeta}(t,y)}}{e^{\alpha\Phi_{\zeta}(s,x)}}\,p^{BM}_{s,t}(x,y),\qquad p^{BM}_{s,t}(x,y)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(\xi^{\prime}(t)-\xi^{\prime}(s))}}\exp\!\left(-\frac{(y-x)^{2}}{2(\xi^{\prime}(t)-\xi^{\prime}(s))}\right).

Proof.

Set
\alpha:=\zeta([0,s])
and define the Hopf–Cole transform

H(u,y):=\exp\bigl(\alpha\,\Phi_{\zeta}(u,y)\bigr).

Since
\zeta((s,t))=0
, we have
\zeta([0,u])=\alpha
for all
u\in[s,t)
. Multiplying the Parisi PDE
by
\alpha
and using the identities

\partial_{u}H=\alpha H\,\partial_{u}\Phi_{\zeta},\qquad\partial_{yy}H=\alpha H\,\partial_{yy}\Phi_{\zeta}+\alpha^{2}H\,(\partial_{y}\Phi_{\zeta})^{2},

one checks that
H
solves the backward heat equation on
[s,t]
:

(B.12)

\partial_{u}H(u,y)+\frac{1}{2}\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(u)\,\partial_{yy}H(u,y)=0,\qquad u\in[s,t).

Let
(Y_{u})_{u\in[s,t]}
be the inhomogeneous Brownian motion started at
Y_{s}=x
,

\mathrm{d}Y_{u}=\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(u)}\,\mathrm{d}W_{u},\qquad Y_{s}=x,

defined on a filtered probability space with law denoted
P^{BM}_{s,x}
. By Itô’s formula and
(
B.12
), the process
(H(u,Y_{u}))_{u\in[s,t]}
is a positive
P^{BM}_{s,x}
–martingale.
Define a new probability measure
Q_{s,x}
on
\mathcal{F}_{t}
by

(B.13)

\frac{\mathrm{d}Q_{s,x}}{\mathrm{d}P^{BM}_{s,x}}\Big|_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}=\frac{H(t,Y_{t})}{H(s,x)}.

Standard Doob
h
–transform (equivalently, Girsanov’s theorem applied to (
B.13
))
implies that under
Q_{s,x}
, the process
(Y_{u})_{u\in[s,t]}
satisfies

\mathrm{d}Y_{u}=\xi^{\prime\prime}(u)\,\partial_{y}\log H(u,Y_{u})\,\mathrm{d}u+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(u)}\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{W}_{u}=\alpha\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(u)\,\partial_{y}\Phi_{\zeta}(u,Y_{u})\,\mathrm{d}u+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(u)}\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{W}_{u},

where
(\widetilde{W}_{u})
is a
Q_{s,x}
–Brownian motion. Since
\zeta([0,u])=\alpha
for
u\in[s,t]
,
this is precisely the Auffinger–Chen dynamics on the plateau
[s,t]
. Consequently, the conditional law of

(X_{u})_{u\in[s,t]}
given
X_{s}=x
coincides with the law of
(Y_{u})_{u\in[s,t]}
under
Q_{s,x}
.

In particular, for any bounded measurable
f
,

\mathbb{E}\bigl[f(X_{t})\mid X_{s}=x\bigr]=\mathbb{E}_{Q_{s,x}}\bigl[f(Y_{t})\bigr]=\mathbb{E}_{P^{BM}_{s,x}}\!\left[\frac{H(t,Y_{t})}{H(s,x)}\,f(Y_{t})\right]=\frac{1}{H(s,x)}\int_{\mathbb{R}}H(t,y)\,f(y)\,p^{BM}_{s,t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Therefore the conditional transition density satisfies

p_{s,t}(x,y)=\frac{H(t,y)}{H(s,x)}\,p^{BM}_{s,t}(x,y)=\frac{e^{\alpha\Phi_{\zeta}(t,y)}}{e^{\alpha\Phi_{\zeta}(s,x)}}\,p^{BM}_{s,t}(x,y),

and substituting the explicit Gaussian form of
p^{BM}_{s,t}
yields the stated proportionality formula.
∎

Lemma B.4
.

Let
(X_{t})
solve the Auffinger–Chen SDE with
X_{0}=0
and set
M_{t}:=\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})
.
Let
0\leq s<t\leq 1
.

(i)

(B.14)

\mathbb{E}[(X_{t}-X_{s})M_{s}]=\mathbb{E}[M_{s}^{2}]\int_{s}^{t}\zeta([0,u])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(u)\,\mathrm{d}u.

(ii)

(B.15)

\mathbb{E}[(M_{t}-M_{s})\,X_{s}]=0.

(iii)

(B.16)

\mathbb{E}[X_{t}M_{t}]=\xi^{\prime}(t)-\int_{[0,1]}\xi^{\prime}\bigl(\min(t,u)\bigr)\,\mathbb{E}[M_{u}^{2}]\;\zeta(\mathrm{d}u).

Consequently, for all
s,t\in[0,1]
,

(B.17)

\mathbb{E}[X_{s}\,M_{t}]=\begin{cases}\mathbb{E}[X_{s}M_{s}],&s\leq t,\\[4.0pt]
\mathbb{E}[X_{t}M_{t}]+\mathbb{E}[M_{t}^{2}]\displaystyle\int_{t}^{s}\zeta([0,u])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(u)\,\mathrm{d}u,&s\geq t.\end{cases}

(iv)

Suppose that
\mathbb{E}[\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})^{2}]=t
for
\zeta
-a.e.
t\in[0,1]
. Suppose
\zeta((s,t))=0
and
s,t\in\mathrm{supp}(\zeta)
. Then

(B.18)

\mathbb{E}[(M_{t}-M_{s})(X_{t}-X_{s})]=\bigl(\xi^{\prime}(t)-\xi^{\prime}(s)\bigr)\int_{s}^{1}\zeta([0,u])\,\mathrm{d}u.

Proof.

Throughout,
(M_{t})
is a martingale by Lemma
B.1
(ii)
.

Proof of
(i)
.

From the SDE,
X_{t}-X_{s}=\int_{s}^{t}\zeta([0,u])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(u)\,M_{u}\,\mathrm{d}u+\int_{s}^{t}\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(u)}\,\mathrm{d}B_{u}
.
Multiplying by
M_{s}
and taking expectations, the stochastic integral vanishes
(being independent of
\mathcal{F}_{s}
), and the martingale property

\mathbb{E}[M_{u}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s}]=M_{s}
for
u\geq s
gives

\mathbb{E}[(X_{t}-X_{s})M_{s}]=\int_{s}^{t}\zeta([0,u])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(u)\,\mathbb{E}[M_{s}\,M_{u}]\,\mathrm{d}u=\mathbb{E}[M_{s}^{2}]\int_{s}^{t}\zeta([0,u])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(u)\,\mathrm{d}u.

Proof of
(ii)
.

Since
X_{s}
is
\mathcal{F}_{s}
-measurable and
\mathbb{E}[M_{t}-M_{s}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s}]=0
, this is immediate.

Proof of
(iii)
.

By Itô’s product rule,

\mathrm{d}(X_{t}M_{t})=M_{t}\,\mathrm{d}X_{t}+X_{t}\,\mathrm{d}M_{t}+\mathrm{d}[X,M]_{t}.

Using
\mathrm{d}M_{t}=\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\,\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}

and
\mathrm{d}[X,M]_{t}=\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t
,
the drift of
\mathrm{d}(X_{t}M_{t})
is

\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\Bigl(\zeta([0,t])\,M_{t}^{2}+\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t.

Taking expectations and integrating from
0
to
t
(with
X_{0}=0
):

(B.19)

\mathbb{E}[X_{t}M_{t}]=\int_{0}^{t}\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)\Bigl(\zeta([0,s])\,\mathbb{E}[M_{s}^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(s,X_{s})]\Bigr)\mathrm{d}s.

By Lemma
B.2
,

\mathbb{E}[\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(s,X_{s})]=1-\zeta\bigl([0,s)\bigr)\,\mathbb{E}[M_{s}^{2}]-\int_{[s,1]}\mathbb{E}[M_{u}^{2}]\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}u).

Since
\zeta([0,s])-\zeta([0,s))=\zeta(\{s\})=0
for Lebesgue-a.e.
s
,
the integrand in (
B.19
) simplifies
\mathrm{d}s
-a.e. to

\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)\Bigl(1-\int_{s}^{1}\mathbb{E}[M_{u}^{2}]\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}u)\Bigr).

Hence

\mathbb{E}[X_{t}M_{t}]=\xi^{\prime}(t)-\int_{0}^{t}\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)\int_{s}^{1}\mathbb{E}[M_{u}^{2}]\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}u)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Exchanging the order of integration, the inner Lebesgue integral
over
s\in[0,\min(t,u)]
evaluates to
\xi^{\prime}(\min(t,u))
, yielding
(
B.16
).

The formula (
B.17
) follows: the case
s\leq t
is

(ii)
; the case
s\geq t
combines
\mathbb{E}[X_{s}M_{t}]=\mathbb{E}[X_{t}M_{t}]+\mathbb{E}[(X_{s}-X_{t})M_{t}]

with
(i)
.

Proof of
(iv)
.

By
(i)
and
(ii)
,

(B.20)

\mathbb{E}[(M_{t}-M_{s})(X_{t}-X_{s})]=\mathbb{E}[M_{t}(X_{t}-X_{s})]-\mathbb{E}[M_{s}(X_{t}-X_{s})]\\
=\bigl(\mathbb{E}[X_{t}M_{t}]-\mathbb{E}[X_{s}M_{s}]\bigr)-\mathbb{E}[M_{s}^{2}]\int_{s}^{t}\zeta([0,u])\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(u)\,\mathrm{d}u,

where we used
\mathbb{E}[M_{t}X_{s}]=\mathbb{E}[M_{s}X_{s}]
from
(ii)
.
Since
\zeta((s,t))=0
, we have
\zeta([0,u])=\zeta([0,s])=:m
for
u\in[s,t)
and

\int_{[r,1]}\mathbb{E}[M_{u}^{2}]\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}u)
is constant in
r\in(s,t)
.
From the proof of
(iii)
, the difference reduces to

\mathbb{E}[X_{t}M_{t}]-\mathbb{E}[X_{s}M_{s}]=\bigl(\xi^{\prime}(t)-\xi^{\prime}(s)\bigr)\Bigl(1-\int_{[t,1]}\mathbb{E}[M_{u}^{2}]\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}u)\Bigr).

Subtracting
\mathbb{E}[M_{s}^{2}]\cdot m\bigl(\xi^{\prime}(t)-\xi^{\prime}(s)\bigr)
and
using
s,t\in\mathrm{supp}(\zeta)
so that
\mathbb{E}[M_{s}^{2}]=s
(by assumption and since
s\mapsto\mathbb{E}[M_{s}^{2}]
is continuous), we get

1-\int_{t}^{1}\mathbb{E}[M_{u}^{2}]\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}u)=\mathbb{E}[\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X_{t})]+\zeta([0,t))\,t=\int_{t}^{1}\zeta([0,u])\,\mathrm{d}u+mt.

Substituting, we obtain

\mathbb{E}[(M_{t}-M_{s})(X_{t}-X_{s})]=\bigl(\xi^{\prime}(t)-\xi^{\prime}(s)\bigr)\Bigl(\int_{t}^{1}\zeta([0,u])\,\mathrm{d}u+mt-sm\Bigr)=\bigl(\xi^{\prime}(t)-\xi^{\prime}(s)\bigr)\int_{s}^{1}\zeta([0,u])\,\mathrm{d}u,

since
\zeta([0,u])=m
on
[s,t)
.
∎

Lemma B.5

(Parisi flow preserves convexity)
.

Let
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be
C^{2}
with
g^{\prime\prime}\geq\kappa
for some
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
, and let

\Phi_{\zeta}
be the solution of the Parisi PDE run backward on
[t,1]
with terminal
condition
\Phi_{\zeta}(1,\cdot)=g
, where
\xi^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
and
\zeta\geq 0
.
Then for every
s\in[t,1]
,

\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(s,x)\;\geq\;\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,g^{\prime\prime}(x)\;\geq\;\kappa.

In particular,
x\mapsto\Phi_{\zeta}(s,x)
is everywhere at least as convex as
g
.

Proof.

Step 1: step-function
\zeta
.

On a layer
[a,b]
where
t\mapsto\zeta([0,t])
is constant equal to
m
, set
\sigma^{2}:=\int_{a}^{b}\xi^{\prime\prime}(r)\,dr

and let
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. The corresponding backward map is

\mathcal{T}_{m,\sigma}(f)(x):=\begin{cases}\mathbb{E}\,f(x+\sigma Z),&m=0,\\[4.0pt]
\dfrac{1}{m}\log\mathbb{E}\exp\!\bigl\{mf(x+\sigma Z)\bigr\},&m>0.\end{cases}

Suppose
f^{\prime\prime}\geq\kappa
. For
m=0
the claim is immediate:

(\mathcal{T}_{0,\sigma}f)^{\prime\prime}(x)=\mathbb{E}f^{\prime\prime}(x+\sigma Z)\geq\kappa
.
For
m>0
, let
Q_{x}
denote the probability measure whose density (with respect to the
law of
Z
) is proportional to
e^{mf(x+\sigma Z)}
. A direct computation gives

(\mathcal{T}_{m,\sigma}f)^{\prime\prime}(x)=\mathbb{E}_{Q_{x}}\!\bigl[f^{\prime\prime}(x+\sigma Z)\bigr]+m\,\mathrm{Var}_{Q_{x}}\!\bigl(f^{\prime}(x+\sigma Z)\bigr)\;\geq\;\kappa,

where the inequality uses
f^{\prime\prime}\geq\kappa
for the first term and nonnegativity of
variance for the second. Hence every layer preserves the lower curvature bound
\kappa
.
For a step function
\zeta
, the solution satisfies

\Phi_{\zeta}(s,\cdot)=\mathcal{T}_{m_{1},\sigma_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{T}_{m_{p},\sigma_{p}}(g)

over the layers covering
[s,1]
, so
\inf_{x}\,\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(s,x)\geq\kappa

follows by induction.

Step 2: general
\zeta
.

For general
\zeta
, choose probability measures
\zeta_{n}
with finitely many atoms such that
\zeta_{n}\to\zeta
.
The corresponding solutions
\Phi_{\zeta_{n}}
converge to
\Phi_{\zeta}
locally uniformly
(see, e.g.,
[
27
, Proposition 2.3]
).
Since each
\Phi_{\zeta_{n}}(s,\cdot)-\frac{\kappa}{2}(\cdot)^{2}
is convex, so is its locally uniform limit
\Phi_{\zeta}(s,\cdot)-\frac{\kappa}{2}(\cdot)^{2}
, which gives
\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(s,\cdot)\geq\kappa
.
∎

B.2.
Optimality conditions for the TAP correction

In this subsection, we study the variational problem
\zeta\mapsto\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\zeta)
.

Recall that we abuse notation and write
\zeta([0,t])
for
\zeta([q,t])
when
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
and
t\in[q,1]
.

Lemma B.6
.

Let
q\in[0,1)
,
\mathbf{m}\in(-1,1)^{N}
, and set
\mu:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{m_{i}}\in\mathcal{P}((-1,1))
. Let
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
and let
(X_{t}^{\mu})_{t\in[q,1]}
be the Auffinger–Chen process with law-matching condition

(B.21)

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{\mu}=\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\zeta([0,t])\,\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,X^{\mu}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t},\\
\mathrm{Law}\bigl(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,X^{\mu}_{q})\bigr)=\mu.\end{cases}

Recall
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\zeta)
from (
1.8
). The following assertions hold.

(i)

The map
\zeta\mapsto\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\zeta)
is strictly convex on
\mathcal{P}([q,1])
.

(ii)

For every finite signed measure
\eta
on
[q,1]
with
\eta([q,1])=0
, the Gâteaux derivative of
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\cdot)
at
\zeta
in the direction
\eta
is given by

(B.22)

D\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\cdot)(\zeta)[\eta]=\frac{1}{2}\int_{q}^{1}\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)\Bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(s,X^{\mu}_{s}))^{2}\bigr]-s\Bigr)\,\eta([0,s])\,\mathrm{d}s.

(iii)

For every
s\in[q,1]
, define

(B.23)

H^{\mu}_{\zeta}(s):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{s}^{1}\xi^{\prime\prime}(r)\Bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(r,X^{\mu}_{r}))^{2}\bigr]-r\Bigr)\mathrm{d}r,

and extend it to
[0,1]
by setting

(B.24)

\hat{H}_{\zeta}^{\mu}(s):=\begin{cases}{H}_{\zeta}^{\mu}(q)&\text{if $s\in[0,q)$,}\\
{H}_{\zeta}^{\mu}(s)&\text{if $s\in[q,1]$.}\end{cases}

Then
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
is the unique minimizer of
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\cdot)
if and only if for every
\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
,

\int_{q}^{1}\hat{H}^{\mu}_{\zeta}(s)\,\mathrm{d}\zeta^{\prime}(s)\geq\int_{q}^{1}\hat{H}^{\mu}_{\zeta}(s)\,\mathrm{d}\zeta(s).

Equivalently,
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
is the unique minimizer of
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\cdot)
if and only if

(B.25)

\mathrm{supp}(\zeta)\subset\underset{[0,1]}{\mathrm{arg\,min}}\,\hat{H}_{\zeta}^{\mu}.

(iv)

Let
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
be the unique minimizer of
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\cdot)
. Then, for every
q^{\prime}\in\mathrm{supp}(\zeta)
,

(B.26)

\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q^{\prime},X^{\mu}_{q^{\prime}}))^{2}\bigr]=q^{\prime}.

(For interior points
q^{\prime}\in(q,1)
this is a first-order optimality condition; at the boundary
q^{\prime}=q
it follows from the law-matching condition (
B.21
), since
\mathbb{E}[(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,X_{q}^{\mu}))^{2}]=\int m^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(m)=q
.)

(v)

Let
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
be the unique minimizer of
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\cdot)
. Then, for every
q^{\prime}\in\mathrm{supp}(\zeta)
,

(B.27)

\xi^{\prime\prime}(q^{\prime})\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(q^{\prime},X^{\mu}_{q^{\prime}}))^{2}\bigr]\leq 1.

(vi)

Let
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
be the unique minimizer of
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\cdot)
. Then

(B.28)

q\in\mathrm{supp}(\zeta).

Remark B.1
.

Notice the crucial property that

\mathrm{TAP}(\delta_{0},\zeta)=\Phi_{\zeta}(0,0)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}t\xi^{\prime\prime}(t)\zeta([0,t])\mathrm{d}t=\mathcal{P}\mathrm{arisi}(\zeta).

Therefore, the minimizer of the right-hand side of the last display satisfies (
B.26
), (
B.27
) and (
B.28
).

Proof.

Assertion
(i)
follows from
[
18
, Lemma 42]
.

Proof of
(ii)
.
Set
m(s)=\zeta([0,s])
. Fix a finite signed measure
\eta
on
[q,1]
with
\eta([q,1])=0
and set
h(s):=\eta([0,s])
,
\zeta_{\varepsilon}:=\zeta+\varepsilon\eta
,
m_{\varepsilon}:=m+\varepsilon h
,
\Phi_{\varepsilon}:=\Phi_{\zeta_{\varepsilon}}
,
u_{\varepsilon}:=\partial_{x}\Phi_{\varepsilon}
, and
u:=\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}
. Define the difference quotient

\Delta_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{\Phi_{\varepsilon}-\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon},\qquad\Delta_{\varepsilon}(1,\cdot)=0,

where
\Phi_{0}:=\Phi_{\zeta}
. One can check that
\Delta_{\varepsilon}
converges locally uniformly to the solution
\Psi
of

\partial_{s}\Psi=-\tfrac{1}{2}\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)\Big(\partial_{xx}\Psi+2m(s)\,u(s,\cdot)\,\partial_{x}\Psi+h(s)\,u(s,\cdot)^{2}\Big),\qquad\Psi(1,\cdot)=0.

By the Feynman–Kac representation theorem applied to the operator

\mathcal{L}_{s}f=\tfrac{1}{2}\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)\bigl(\partial_{xx}f+2m(s)\,u(s,\cdot)\,\partial_{x}f\bigr),

for every
s\in[q,1]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

(B.29)

\Psi(s,x)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{s}^{1}\xi^{\prime\prime}(r)\,h(r)\,\mathbb{E}_{s,x}\bigl[(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(r,X_{r}))^{2}\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}r,

where
\mathbb{E}_{s,x}
denotes the expectation for the diffusion with generator
\mathcal{L}_{s}
started at position
x
at time
s
.

We now differentiate the Legendre transform term. Recall that

\Phi_{\zeta_{\varepsilon}}^{*}(q,y)=\sup_{x}\bigl\{xy-\Phi_{\zeta_{\varepsilon}}(q,x)\bigr\}.

By the envelope theorem (the supremum being attained at a unique point by strict convexity of
\Phi_{\zeta}(q,\cdot)
), the derivative with respect to
\varepsilon
at
\varepsilon=0
is computed by differentiating only the integrand at the optimal
x
. For every
m^{\prime}\in(-1,1)
, the supremum defining
\Phi_{\zeta}^{*}(q,m^{\prime})
is attained at
x(m^{\prime}):=(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,\cdot))^{-1}(m^{\prime})
, hence

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}\Phi_{\zeta_{\varepsilon}}^{*}(q,m^{\prime})=-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}\Phi_{\zeta_{\varepsilon}}(q,x(m^{\prime}))=-\Psi(q,x(m^{\prime})).

Averaging against
\mu
gives

(B.30)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}\int\Phi_{\zeta_{\varepsilon}}^{*}(q,m^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\mu(m^{\prime})=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Psi(q,x(m_{i})).

By the construction of the Auffinger–Chen process (
B.21
), the law of
X^{\mu}_{q}
is chosen so that
\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,X_{q}^{\mu})\sim\mu
, which implies
X_{q}^{\mu}\sim(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,\cdot))^{-1}\#\mu
. Therefore,

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Psi(q,x(m_{i}))=\mathbb{E}[\Psi(q,X^{\mu}_{q})].

Inserting (
B.29
) (with
s=q
) into (
B.30
), we obtain

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}\int\Phi_{\zeta_{\varepsilon}}^{*}(q,m^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\mu(m^{\prime})=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{q}^{1}\xi^{\prime\prime}(r)\,h(r)\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(r,X^{\mu}_{r}))^{2}\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}r.

Adding the derivative of the linear term
-\frac{1}{2}\int_{q}^{1}s\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)\,h(s)\,\mathrm{d}s
yields (
B.22
).

Proof of
(iii)
.

By Fubini’s theorem, one can rewrite (
B.22
) as

D\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\cdot)(\zeta)[\eta]=\int_{[q,1]}H_{\zeta}^{\mu}(s)\,\mathrm{d}\eta(s)+H^{\mu}_{\zeta}(q)\,\eta([0,q)).

By the strict convexity established in
(i)
, the measure
\zeta
minimizes
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\cdot)
over
\mathcal{P}([q,1])
if and only if
D\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\cdot)(\zeta)[\zeta^{\prime}-\zeta]\geq 0
for every
\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
, that is,

\int_{[q,1]}H^{\mu}_{\zeta}(s)\,\mathrm{d}\zeta^{\prime}(s)+H_{\zeta}^{\mu}(q)\,\zeta^{\prime}([0,q))\geq\int_{[q,1]}H^{\mu}_{\zeta}(s)\,\mathrm{d}\zeta(s)+H_{\zeta}^{\mu}(q)\,\zeta([0,q)).

Recalling that
\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
(so both may charge
[0,q)
through their definition on the full interval, but here
\zeta^{\prime}([0,q))=0
and
\zeta([0,q))=0
), the terms involving
\eta([0,q))
vanish. More precisely, introducing

\hat{H}_{\zeta}^{\mu}(s):=\begin{cases}{H}_{\zeta}^{\mu}(q)&\text{if $s\in[0,q)$,}\\
{H}_{\zeta}^{\mu}(s)&\text{if $s\in[q,1]$,}\end{cases}

the optimality condition reads: for every
\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
,

\int_{q}^{1}\hat{H}^{\mu}_{\zeta}(s)\,\mathrm{d}\zeta^{\prime}(s)\geq\int_{q}^{1}\hat{H}^{\mu}_{\zeta}(s)\,\mathrm{d}\zeta(s).

The equivalence with the support condition (
B.25
) is classical: the above condition states that
\zeta
minimizes the linear functional
\zeta^{\prime}\mapsto\int\hat{H}^{\mu}_{\zeta}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta^{\prime}
over
\mathcal{P}([q,1])
, which holds if and only if
\zeta
is supported on the set of minimizers of
\hat{H}^{\mu}_{\zeta}
over
[0,1]
.

Proof of
(iv)
.

Since
q^{\prime}\in\mathrm{supp}(\zeta)\subset\mathrm{arg\,min}\,\hat{H}_{\zeta}^{\mu}
by (
B.25
) and
q^{\prime}\in[q,1]
, the function
H_{\zeta}^{\mu}
attains its minimum at
q^{\prime}
. In particular
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}H_{\zeta}^{\mu}(s)\big|_{s=q^{\prime}}=0
. Differentiating (
B.23
) gives

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}H_{\zeta}^{\mu}(s)=\frac{1}{2}\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)\Bigl(s-\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(s,X^{\mu}_{s}))^{2}\bigr]\Bigr).

Since
\xi^{\prime\prime}(q^{\prime})>0
, the vanishing of this derivative at
s=q^{\prime}
yields (
B.26
).

Proof of
(v)
.

As in the proof of
(iv)
,
H_{\zeta}^{\mu}
attains its minimum at every
q^{\prime}\in\mathrm{supp}(\zeta)
, so
\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}H_{\zeta}^{\mu}(s)\big|_{s=q^{\prime}}\geq 0
(at the boundary
q^{\prime}=q
, this is the right second derivative, which is well-defined since the first-order condition (
B.26
) holds and
H_{\zeta}^{\mu}
is smooth on
(q,q+\delta)
for small
\delta
). Set
u(s,x):=\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(s,x)
. By Lemma
B.1
(iv)
(whose derivation depends only on the Parisi PDE and the drift structure of the process, and therefore applies equally to
(X_{t}^{\mu})
in place of
(X_{t})
),

(B.31)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\mathbb{E}\bigl[u(s,X^{\mu}_{s})^{2}\bigr]=\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(s,X^{\mu}_{s}))^{2}\bigr].

Differentiating (
B.23
) twice and using (
B.26
) (which ensures the first-order term vanishes) together with (
B.31
), we obtain

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}H_{\zeta}^{\mu}(s)\bigg|_{s=q^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{2}\xi^{\prime\prime}(q^{\prime})\bigl(1-\xi^{\prime\prime}(q^{\prime})\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(q^{\prime},X^{\mu}_{q^{\prime}}))^{2}\bigr]\bigr).

Since
\xi^{\prime\prime}(q^{\prime})>0
and
\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}H_{\zeta}^{\mu}(s)\big|_{s=q^{\prime}}\geq 0
, this yields (
B.27
).

Proof of
(vi)
.

This is proved in
[
18
]
; see Theorem 12, item (5) therein.
∎

B.3.
Gradient of the TAP correction

We now compute the gradient of the TAP correction.

Lemma B.7

(Bounds for the defect term)
.

Fix
q\in[0,1)
and let
\mu
be a probability measure on
[-1,1]
with

\int m^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}m)=q
.
For
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
, let
(X_{t}^{\mu,\zeta})_{t\in[q,1]}
be the
Auffinger–Chen process (
B.21
), set

u_{\zeta}(t,x):=\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(t,x),

and define

(B.32)

\Delta_{\zeta}^{\mu}:=\int_{[q,1]}\Bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[u_{\zeta}(t,X_{t}^{\mu,\zeta})^{2}\bigr]-t\Bigr)\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t).

Then:

(i)

For every
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
,

|\Delta_{\zeta}^{\mu}|\leq 1.

(ii)

Let
\zeta_{\mu}
be the unique minimizer of
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu,\cdot)
on
\mathcal{P}([q,1])
.
Assume that
\mu
is supported in
[-1+\delta,1-\delta]
for some

\delta\in(0,1)
, and let
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
satisfy

\zeta(\{q\})\geq u_{0}>0.

Then there exists
C=C(\xi,u_{0})
such that

|\Delta_{\zeta}^{\mu}|\leq\frac{C}{\delta}\,\mathrm{dist}(\zeta,\zeta_{\mu}).

Proof.

For
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}([q,1])
, write

g_{\zeta}(t):=\mathbb{E}\bigl[u_{\zeta}(t,X_{t}^{\mu,\zeta})^{2}\bigr]-t,\qquad t\in[q,1],

so that

\Delta_{\zeta}^{\mu}=\int_{[q,1]}g_{\zeta}(t)\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t).

Proof of
(i)
.

As in Lemma
B.1
(ii)
, the process

u_{\zeta}(t,X_{t}^{\mu,\zeta})
is a martingale on
[q,1]
with terminal value

\tanh(X_{1}^{\mu,\zeta})\in[-1,1]
. Hence

|u_{\zeta}(t,X_{t}^{\mu,\zeta})|\leq 1\qquad\text{a.s. for every }t\in[q,1].

Therefore
|g_{\zeta}(t)|\leq 1
for every
t
, and so

|\Delta_{\zeta}^{\mu}|\leq\int_{[q,1]}|g_{\zeta}(t)|\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t)\leq 1.

Proof of
(ii)
.

Set

\bar{\zeta}:=\zeta_{\mu},\qquad u:=u_{\zeta},\qquad\bar{u}:=u_{\bar{\zeta}},\qquad X:=X^{\mu,\zeta},\qquad\bar{X}:=X^{\mu,\bar{\zeta}},\qquad d:=\mathrm{dist}(\zeta,\bar{\zeta}).

We decompose

\Delta_{\zeta}^{\mu}=\int g_{\bar{\zeta}}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta+\int(g_{\zeta}-g_{\bar{\zeta}})\,\mathrm{d}\zeta.

First we bound the term involving
g_{\bar{\zeta}}
.
By Lemma
B.6
(iv)
,

g_{\bar{\zeta}}(t)=0\qquad\text{for every }t\in\mathrm{supp}(\bar{\zeta}),

hence

\int g_{\bar{\zeta}}\,\mathrm{d}\bar{\zeta}=0.

Also, Lemma
B.5
and Lemma
B.2
(applied at time

s
) imply

0\leq\partial_{x}\bar{u}(s,x)=\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\bar{\zeta}}(s,x)\leq 1\qquad\text{for all }(s,x)\in[q,1]\times\mathbb{R}.

Therefore, exactly as in Lemma
B.1
(iv)
,
for
q\leq s<t\leq 1
,

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bar{u}(t,\bar{X}_{t})^{2}\bigr]-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bar{u}(s,\bar{X}_{s})^{2}\bigr]=\int_{s}^{t}\xi^{\prime\prime}(r)\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\bar{\zeta}}(r,\bar{X}_{r}))^{2}\bigr]\mathrm{d}r,

and hence

\Bigl|\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bar{u}(t,\bar{X}_{t})^{2}\bigr]-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bar{u}(s,\bar{X}_{s})^{2}\bigr]\Bigr|\leq\|\xi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}\,|t-s|.

Thus
g_{\bar{\zeta}}
is Lipschitz on
[q,1]
with

\mathrm{Lip}(g_{\bar{\zeta}})\leq 1+\|\xi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}.

Since
\mathrm{dist}
is the
1
-Wasserstein distance on
[q,1]
, the
Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality gives

\Bigl|\int g_{\bar{\zeta}}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta\Bigr|=\Bigl|\int g_{\bar{\zeta}}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta-\int g_{\bar{\zeta}}\,\mathrm{d}\bar{\zeta}\Bigr|\leq(1+\|\xi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty})\,d.

Next we bound the term involving
g_{\zeta}-g_{\bar{\zeta}}
.
Standard stability estimates for the Parisi PDE
(see, e.g.,
[
18
, Proof of Theorem 12(ii)]
)
give a constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(\xi)
such that

\sup_{(t,x)\in[q,1]\times\mathbb{R}}|u(t,x)-\bar{u}(t,x)|\leq C_{0}\,d.

Assume first that

d\leq\frac{\delta}{2C_{0}}.

Since
\zeta([0,q))=0
, Lemma
B.2
, (
B.7
),
gives for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{x}u(q,x)

\displaystyle=1-\int_{[q,1]}\mathbb{E}\!\left[u(t,X_{t})^{2}\,\middle|\,X_{q}=x\right]\zeta(\mathrm{d}t)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\zeta(\{q\})u(q,x)^{2}-\zeta((q,1])

\displaystyle=\zeta(\{q\})\bigl(1-u(q,x)^{2}\bigr)\geq u_{0}\bigl(1-u(q,x)^{2}\bigr).

Now couple
X
and
\bar{X}
using the same Brownian motion and the same random
variable
M\sim\mu
, by setting

X_{q}=(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,\cdot))^{-1}(M),\qquad\bar{X}_{q}=(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\bar{\zeta}}(q,\cdot))^{-1}(M).

Then

u(q,X_{q})=M=\bar{u}(q,\bar{X}_{q})\qquad\text{a.s.}

Hence

|u(q,\bar{X}_{q})-M|=|u(q,\bar{X}_{q})-\bar{u}(q,\bar{X}_{q})|\leq C_{0}\,d\leq\delta/2.

Because
M\in[-1+\delta,1-\delta]
a.s., both endpoint values

u(q,X_{q})
and
u(q,\bar{X}_{q})
belong to
[-1+\delta/2,1-\delta/2]
.
Since
x\mapsto u(q,x)
is nondecreasing, every value of
u(q,\cdot)
on the
interval between
X_{q}
and
\bar{X}_{q}
also belongs to

[-1+\delta/2,1-\delta/2]
. Therefore, on that interval,

\partial_{x}u(q,\cdot)\geq u_{0}\Bigl(1-(1-\delta/2)^{2}\Bigr)\geq\frac{u_{0}\delta}{2}.

By the mean value theorem,

|X_{q}-\bar{X}_{q}|\leq\frac{2}{u_{0}\delta}\,|u(q,X_{q})-u(q,\bar{X}_{q})|\leq\frac{2C_{0}}{u_{0}\delta}\,d.

Taking expectations gives the same bound for
\mathbb{E}|X_{q}-\bar{X}_{q}|
.

Using the coupled SDEs, the bound
|u|\leq 1
, and the fact that

x\mapsto\bar{u}(s,x)
is
1
-Lipschitz for every
s\in[q,1]
, we obtain for

t\in[q,1]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|X_{t}-\bar{X}_{t}|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}|X_{q}-\bar{X}_{q}|+\|\xi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}\int_{q}^{t}|\zeta([0,s])-\bar{\zeta}([0,s])|\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+\|\xi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}\int_{q}^{t}\Bigl(\sup_{x}|u(s,x)-\bar{u}(s,x)|+\mathbb{E}|X_{s}-\bar{X}_{s}|\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Since

\int_{q}^{1}|\zeta([0,s])-\bar{\zeta}([0,s])|\,\mathrm{d}s=d,

the previous bounds and Grönwall’s lemma yield

\sup_{t\in[q,1]}\mathbb{E}|X_{t}-\bar{X}_{t}|\leq\frac{C_{1}(\xi,u_{0})}{\delta}\,d.

Consequently, for every
t\in[q,1]
,

\displaystyle|g_{\zeta}(t)-g_{\bar{\zeta}}(t)|

\displaystyle=\Bigl|\mathbb{E}\bigl[u(t,X_{t})^{2}-\bar{u}(t,\bar{X}_{t})^{2}\bigr]\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq 2\,\mathbb{E}|u(t,X_{t})-\bar{u}(t,\bar{X}_{t})|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{x}|u(t,x)-\bar{u}(t,x)|+2\,\mathbb{E}|\bar{u}(t,X_{t})-\bar{u}(t,\bar{X}_{t})|

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{0}\,d+2\,\mathbb{E}|X_{t}-\bar{X}_{t}|\leq\frac{C_{2}(\xi,u_{0})}{\delta}\,d.

Hence, when
d\leq\delta/(2C_{0})
,

\Bigl|\int(g_{\zeta}-g_{\bar{\zeta}})\,\mathrm{d}\zeta\Bigr|\leq\sup_{t\in[q,1]}|g_{\zeta}(t)-g_{\bar{\zeta}}(t)|\leq\frac{C_{2}(\xi,u_{0})}{\delta}\,d.

Combining this with the bound on
\int g_{\bar{\zeta}}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta
gives

|\Delta_{\zeta}^{\mu}|\leq(1+\|\xi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty})\,d+\frac{C_{2}(\xi,u_{0})}{\delta}\,d\leq\frac{C(\xi,u_{0})}{\delta}\,d,

since
\delta\leq 1
.

If instead
d>\delta/(2C_{0})
, then part
(i)
yields

|\Delta_{\zeta}^{\mu}|\leq 1\leq\frac{2C_{0}}{\delta}\,d.

This proves part
(ii)
.
∎

Lemma B.8
.

Recall
\mathrm{TAP}
from (
1.9
) and
k_{\zeta}
from (
1.22
).
Fix
\mathbf{m}\in(-1,1)^{N}
and set
q=\frac{1}{N}\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}
.
Let
\zeta
be a probability measure on
[q,1]
. Then for every
i\in[N]
:

(i)

(
Exact gradient at the minimizer
) If
\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}
is the minimizer in Definition
1.1
, then

(B.33)

\partial_{i}\mathrm{TAP}(\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})})=-\frac{1}{N}k_{\zeta_{\mathbf{m}}}(q,m_{i}).

(ii)

(
Exact defect formula and explicit boundary stability
) One has

(B.34)

-N\partial_{i}\mathrm{TAP}(\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})},\zeta)=k_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})-m_{i}\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\,\Delta_{\zeta}^{\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})}},

where
\Delta_{\zeta}^{\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})}}
is defined in (
B.32
).
Consequently, if

\max_{j\in[N]}|m_{j}|\leq 1-\delta\qquad\text{for some }\delta\in(0,1),

and

\zeta(\{q\})\geq u_{0}>0,

then there exists
C=C(\xi,u_{0})<\infty
such that

(B.35)

\Bigl|\partial_{i}\mathrm{TAP}(\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})},\zeta)+\frac{1}{N}k_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})\Bigr|\leq\frac{C}{N\delta}\,\mathrm{dist}(\zeta_{\mathbf{m}},\zeta).

Proof.

Since
q=\frac{1}{N}\|\mathbf{m}\|^{2}
depends on
\mathbf{m}
, we have

\partial_{i}q=\frac{2m_{i}}{N}
. Differentiating
\mathrm{TAP}(\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})},\zeta)

with respect to
m_{i}
by the chain rule yields

(B.36)

-N\partial_{i}\mathrm{TAP}(\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})},\zeta)=\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})+\frac{2m_{i}}{N}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\partial_{q}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{j})+N\,\partial_{q}\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q)\right).

A direct computation gives

\partial_{q}\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}(q)=-\frac{1}{2}\,q\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\,\zeta([0,q]).

Recall that

h_{\zeta}(q,a)=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\bigl(ax-\Phi_{\zeta}(q,x)\bigr),\qquad a\in(-1,1).

Since
\Phi_{\zeta}(q,\cdot)
is strictly convex, the supremum is attained at the
unique point

x(a):=(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,\cdot))^{-1}(a).

By the envelope theorem,

\partial_{q}h_{\zeta}(q,a)=-\partial_{q}\Phi_{\zeta}\bigl(q,x(a)\bigr).

(When
\zeta
has an atom at
q
,
\partial_{q}
denotes the right derivative; the Parisi PDE determines it unambiguously.)
Using the Parisi PDE (
B.4
),

(B.37)

\partial_{q}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,x)=-\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)}{2}\Bigl(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,x)+\zeta([0,q])\,\bigl(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,x)\bigr)^{2}\Bigr),

we obtain

(B.38)

\partial_{q}h_{\zeta}(q,a)=\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)}{2}\Bigl(\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}\bigl(q,x(a)\bigr)+\zeta([0,q])\,a^{2}\Bigr).

Let

\nu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})}:=(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,\cdot))^{-1}\#\,\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})}.

Substituting (
B.38
) into (
B.36
) and using

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}m_{j}^{2}=q
, we get

(B.39)

\displaystyle-N\partial_{i}\mathrm{TAP}(\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})},\zeta)

\displaystyle=\partial_{m}h_{\zeta}(q,m_{i})+m_{i}\,\xi^{\prime\prime}(q)\int\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})}(x).

By Lemma
B.2
,

\int\partial_{xx}\Phi_{\zeta}(q,x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})}(x)=\int_{q}^{1}\zeta([0,s])\,\mathrm{d}s-\int_{[q,1]}\Bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[u_{\zeta}(t,X_{t}^{\mu_{N}^{(\mathbf{m})},\zeta})^{2}\bigr]-t\Bigr)\,\zeta(\mathrm{d}t),

that is,

(B.40)
